Magnetic Double-Wells: Lower Bounds on Tunneling with an Appendix by Tal Shpigel
Charles L. Fefferman, Jacob Shapiro

Abstract
We present lower bounds on tunneling rates in magnetic double well systems for generic values of the coupling constant. This result was recently announced in [ 7 ] and complements our recent counter-example construction [ 6 ] which exhibits vanishing tunneling for specially-constructed double-well potentials.



1
Introduction

Quantum tunneling in double-well systems is a cornerstone phenomenon in mathematical physics. In a non-magnetic setting, a particle localized in one of two deep and well-separated potential wells has a nonzero probability of tunneling to the other well. The tunneling time is inversely proportional to the difference between the lowest two eigenvalues of the double-well Hamiltonian, a quantity
which is (typically) exponentially small in the well-depth and well-separation.
This paradigm, developed both heuristically and rigorously, goes back to the classical literature and underlies a great variety of effects in physics and chemistry; see, for example,
[
16
]
in physics or
[
23
,
10
]
in mathematics. The latter two works derive a lower bound on the eigenvalue splitting assuming the single-well potential has a single non-degenerate minimum. In
[
4
]
lower bounds are derived assuming rather that the single-well is compactly supported.

In contrast, adding a
constant magnetic field
fundamentally alters the picture.
Early works on the magnetic case resolved the case of weak magnetic fields, confirming agreement with the non-magnetic case
[
12
]
. For strong magnetic fields, magnetic translations endow localized states with nontrivial complex phases, yielding interference among tunneling paths. For example in
[
8
]
we showed that for double-well magnetic systems whose single-well is radial, the magnetic field effect leads to an exponentially smaller tunneling amplitude. However, such systems with radial single-well potential were shown to still possess a strictly positive lower bound on tunneling
[
8
,
11
,
19
]
.

Recently, we announced in
[
7
]
and then provided a complete proof in
[
6
]
, that the magnetic setting admits a phenomenon that has no analogue in the non-magnetic case:
exact vanishing of tunneling
in a double-well system built from a suitable non-radial single-well potential.
We constructed a family of (non-radial) single-well potentials such that, in a strong constant magnetic field and for large well depth, the associated symmetric double-well has zero eigenvalue splitting between its two lowest energy levels, so that tunneling is entirely eliminated. Moreover, by varying parameters within this family one can flip the parity of the ground state (from even to odd),
via a coalescence and re-emergence of two distinct eigenvalues. A discussion of implications for potential applications to quantum materials is also discussed in
[
7
]
.

In this paper, we prove a second result announced in
[
7
]
:

The vanishing of the tunneling is a non-generic phenomenon; for couplings,
\lambda>0
, outside of a set of density zero, one obtains a lower bound on the tunneling.

Let us begin by introducing a precise framework. We work with the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. On
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we consider magnetic Schrödinger operators of the form

\displaystyle\left(P-\frac{1}{2}b\lambda X^{\perp}\right)^{2}+\lambda^{2}V(X)\ ,

a perturbation by an electric potential of the
Landau Hamiltonian

H^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda}:=\left(P-\frac{1}{2}b\lambda X^{\perp}\right)^{2}.

(1.1)

Here,
P\equiv-\operatorname{i}\nabla
is the momentum operator,
X
is the position operator, with

\displaystyle X^{\perp}\equiv(-X_{2},X_{1})\,.

The parameter
\lambda>0
is a sufficiently large coupling constant which controls simultaneously the scaling of the magnetic field strength (which grows as
\lambda
), and the depth of the potential
\lambda^{2}V:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to(-\infty,0]
. The parameter
b>0
controls the relative strength of the magnetic field
b\lambda
to the well-depth
\lambda^{2}\min V
.

Such Hamiltonians describe the dynamics of an electron bound to a two-dimensional plane and subject to a constant perpendicular magnetic field. We consider simultaneously two main operators of this type: the
single-well
Hamiltonian
h_{\lambda}
and
double-well
Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
. They are defined as

\displaystyle h_{\lambda,b}:=\left(P-\frac{1}{2}b\lambda X^{\perp}\right)^{2}+\lambda^{2}v(X)

(1.2)

and

\displaystyle H_{\lambda,b}:=\left(P-\frac{1}{2}b\lambda X^{\perp}\right)^{2}+\lambda^{2}v(X+d)+\lambda^{2}v(X-d)\,.

(1.3)

Here
v:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[-1,0]
is a smooth compactly supported (say within
B_{a}(0)
for some
a>0
) single-well potential and
d\equiv(d_{1},0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\Set{0}
is the displacement of each well taken, without loss of generality, to lie along the
1
-axis.

We assume that
v
is chosen so that when
\lambda
is sufficiently large,
h_{\lambda,b}
has ground state energy
e_{0,\lambda}=-\lambda^{2}+\mathrm{o}(\lambda^{2})
which has distance to the remainder of the spectrum with at least order of magnitude
c_{\mathrm{gap}}
, a constant which is independent of
\lambda
. It follows that
E_{0,\lambda}\leq E_{1,\lambda}
, the two lowest eigenvalues of
H_{\lambda}
, are also at a distance of at least order
c_{\rm gap}
from the remainder of the spectrum of
H_{\lambda}
. We are chiefly concerned with the
double-well eigenvalue splitting
defined by

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}(\lambda):=E_{1,\lambda}-E_{0,\lambda}\,.

An emergent quantity related to
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)
is the
hopping coefficient
, which will also play a role in the present analysis. Its definition is given by

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\widehat{R}^{-d}\varphi_{0,\lambda}\left(H_{\lambda}-e_{0,\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\widehat{R}^{d}\varphi_{0,\lambda}\right\rangle

(1.4)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\overline{\varphi_{0,\lambda}(x+d)}v(x+d)\exp\left(\operatorname{i}b\lambda d_{1}x_{2}\right)\varphi_{0,\lambda}(x-d)\operatorname{d}{x}

Here
\varphi_{0,\lambda}
is the
L^{2}-
normalized ground state of
h_{\lambda}
;
\widehat{R}^{d}
is Zak’s magnetic translation operator
[
27
]
, given by

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{z}:=\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}z\cdot\left(P+\frac{1}{2}b\lambda X^{\perp}\right)\right)\qquad(z\in\mathbb{R}^{2})\,.

(1.5)

Since
z\cdot x^{\perp}=-z^{\perp}\cdot x
,

\displaystyle(\widehat{R}^{z}f)(x)\equiv\exp\left(\operatorname{i}\frac{b\lambda}{2}x\cdot z^{\perp}\right)f(x-z)\qquad(x,z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,,f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C})\,.

Below we use the notion of
a set of density zero
; we say that
S\subseteq(0,\infty)
has
zero density
iff

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\frac{\mu\left(S\cap[0,M]\right)}{M}=0

where
\mu
is the Lebesgue measure.

Our main theorem is

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
v:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[-1,0]
be of class
C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and supported within
B_{a}(0)
, where
a>0
. Further assume that
v(0)=-1
is a unique global minimum which is non-degenerate. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Assume that
2d_{1}>Ca
for some
C
sufficiently large but independent of
\lambda
, where
d=(d_{1},0)
is the displacement parameter of the double-well; see
eq.

1.3
.

Then, there is a constant
b_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for all
b\in(0,b_{\varepsilon})
, the following holds:

1.

There exists a set of density zero
Z(\varepsilon,v)\subseteq(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\left|\rho_{0}(\lambda)\right|\geq\exp\left(-\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\right)\quad\textrm{for all}\quad\lambda\in(0,\infty)\setminus Z(\varepsilon,v)\ ,

(1.6)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}(\lambda)\geq\exp\left(-\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\right)\quad\textrm{for all}\quad\lambda\in(0,\infty)\setminus Z(\varepsilon,v)\ .

(1.7)

2.

In fact, there exists some
\Lambda<\infty
and a discrete set
\Set{\lambda_{k}}_{k\geq 1}\subseteq(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}^{-\left(1+\varepsilon\right)}<\infty\qquad(\varepsilon>0)\,.

(1.8)

and such that
\rho_{0}
and
\Delta_{0}
are non-zero on
\left[\Lambda,\infty\right)\setminus\Set{\lambda_{k}}_{k}
.

3.

Averaged counterpart to item 1
:

For any
\varepsilon>0
there exists some
b_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for all
b\in(0,b_{\varepsilon})
and some
C(\varepsilon,b,d_{1})<\infty
such that there exists some
\lambda_{\star}(\varepsilon)<\infty
such that for all
\lambda\geq\lambda_{\star}(\varepsilon)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\lambda}^{2\lambda}-\log\left(\left|\rho_{0}(\widetilde{\lambda})\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{\widetilde{\lambda}}\leq C(\varepsilon,b,d_{1})\ \lambda^{1+\varepsilon}

(1.9)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\lambda}^{2\lambda}-\log\left(\left|\Delta_{0}(\widetilde{\lambda})\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{\widetilde{\lambda}}\leq C(\varepsilon,b,d_{1})\ \lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\,.

(1.10)

Remark 1.2
.

Throughout our analysis,
\varepsilon>0
is fixed once and for all as an arbitrarily small independent parameter. One may be tempted to take
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
as
\lambda\to\infty
, for example, examining the role of
\varepsilon
below in
eq.

2.23
one may be tempted to take
\varepsilon\sim\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}
which would have improved our lower bounds to

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}(\lambda)\gtrsim\exp\left(-c\lambda\right)\left(1+o(1)\right)\qquad(\lambda\to\infty)\,.

Unfortunately this is not possible
throughout
. See e.g.,
section

4
. There, we need to take
b
sufficiently small dependent on
\varepsilon
(in fact
\varepsilon\sim b
) uniformly in
\lambda
.

Remark 1.3
.

Instead of assuming that
\left|\arg(\lambda)\right|<\frac{\pi}{2}-\varepsilon
and
b\in(0,b_{\varepsilon})
, let us suppose that
b\in(0,b_{\rm max})
and
\left|\arg(\lambda)\right|<\varepsilon(b_{\rm max})
, with
b_{\rm max}
large and
\varepsilon(b_{\rm max})\ll 1
.

Then, we believe that our analytic continuation arguments would go through with small changes. In place of upper bounds of the form
\exp\left(\left|\lambda\right|^{1+\varepsilon}\right)
on the relevant analytic function, we would find instead upper bounds
\exp\left(\left|\lambda\right|^{K(b_{\rm max})}\right)
for a constant
K(b_{\rm max})\gg 1
.

Unless
\rho_{0}(\lambda)
and
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)
vanish identically for the given
b
, that would imply lower bounds of the form

\displaystyle\left|\rho_{0}(\lambda)\right|,\left|\Delta_{0}(\lambda)\right|>\exp\left(-\lambda^{K(b_{\rm max})}\right)

for
\lambda
outside a set of density zero.

Joint analyticity in
(b,\lambda)
for
\left|\arg(\lambda)\right|,\left|\arg(b)\right|<\varepsilon(b_{\rm max})
would imply that
\rho_{0}(\lambda),\Delta_{0}(\lambda)
vanish identically as a function of
\lambda
at most for finitely many
b\in(0,b_{\rm max})
.

We haven’t carried this out, and we know no way to rule out the existence of
b
for which, say,
\rho_{0}(\lambda)=0
for all large
\lambda
.

1.1
Rough sketch of the proof

(1)

The key is to prove that, up to a
\lambda
-dependent normalization factor,
\rho_{0}(\lambda,b)
and
\Delta_{0}(\lambda,b)
are analytic in a region

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}:=\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}}{\Lambda<\left|z\right|\quad{\rm and}\quad\left|\arg\left(z\right)\right|<\frac{\pi}{2}-\varepsilon}\,.

contained within the open right half plane. Here
\varepsilon>0
is a fixed arbitrarily small parameter and
\Lambda>0
is chosen sufficiently large after a finite number of conditions to be specified below. Further, we show that the above functions are bounded from above
on
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
by
\lambda\mapsto\left|\lambda\right|^{k}\exp(Cb\left|\lambda\right|)
for some universal
k,C
.

(2)

Fix some
\lambda_{\star}\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R}
. Then, by lower bounds of hopping coefficients and the splitting in
non-magnetic systems
,
[
23
,
10
,
4
]
, we have strictly positive lower
bounds on
|\rho_{0}(\lambda_{\star},b=0)|
and

|\Delta_{0}(\lambda_{\star},b=0)|
. Therefore, by continuity, we have strictly positive lower bounds on

|\rho_{0}(\lambda_{\star},b)|
and

|\Delta_{0}(\lambda_{\star},b)|
for all
b\in(0,b_{\star})
with
b_{\star}
sufficiently small (dependent on the choice of
\lambda_{\star}
).

(3)

Given (1) and (2) we apply the following complex analysis lemma (see
3.10
below), together with bounds on the normalization factor for real
\lambda
, to establish that for fixed
b\in(0,b_{\star})
,
|\rho_{0}(\lambda,b)|
and
|\Delta_{0}(\lambda,b)|
satisfy the desired lower bounds for all real
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R}
except possibly for
\lambda
in an exceptional set of density zero.

Lemma 1.4

(Complex analysis lemma)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1]
and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha}:=\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}:-\alpha\pi/2<\operatorname{Arg}(z)<\alpha\pi/2}\,.

Here
\operatorname{Arg}(z)
is the principal value, taking values
-\pi<\operatorname{Arg}(z)\leq\pi
. Let
F:\Gamma_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic such that for some given
\beta\in(0,\infty)
we have

\displaystyle\left|F(1)\right|\geq\operatorname{e}^{-\beta}

and such that for some analytic
nowhere-zero
function
U:\Gamma_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{C}
we have

\displaystyle\left|F(z)\right|\leq\left|U(z)\right|\qquad(z\in\Gamma_{\alpha})\,.

Then there is constant
C<\infty
(independent of
\alpha,F
and
\beta
) such that for all
R>2^{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha 2^{1/\alpha}\left(\beta+\log\left(\left|U(1)\right|\right)\right)R^{1/\alpha}+

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|U(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\,.

as well as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}\left|-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)+\log\left(\left|U(t)\right|\right)-t^{1/\alpha}\mu_{0}\right|\operatorname{d}{t}\overset{R\to\infty}{=}o\left(R^{1/\alpha}\right)

for some
\mu_{0}\geq 0
.

(4)

Hence the proof of
1.1
boils down to establishing analytic continuation properties of
\rho_{0}(\lambda)
and
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)
to
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
, as well as upper bounds.

(5)

An obstruction to analyticity?
We first observe that for real
\lambda
large, the ground state of
h_{\lambda}
,
\varphi_{\lambda}
, is well-approximated by the normalized ground state,
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}
, of the magnetic harmonic oscillator Hamiltonian. Therefore,
for all
\lambda
real and sufficiently large,
\varphi_{\lambda}
, the ground state of
h_{\lambda}
is given by a Riesz projection: a contour integral of
\zeta\mapsto\big(h_{\lambda}-\zeta\mathds{1}\big)^{-1}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}
(up to normalization). In the contour integral,
\zeta
varies over a circle centered at
e_{\lambda}
of radius say half the distance to the second eigenvalue of
h_{\lambda}
.

Now, while
\lambda\mapsto\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}
has an analytic continuation for
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
, the mapping

\lambda\mapsto\big(h_{\lambda}-\zeta\mathds{1}\big)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, does
not
. Indeed,
h_{\lambda}
is a relatively compact perturbation of
H^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda}
, and the resolvent of
the latter does not have an analytic continuation off the real axis; see
[
15
]
and the discussion in
C.2
.

(6)

The remedy:
We note that the above Riesz projection representation of
\varphi_{\lambda}
, up to normalization, works just as well if we replace
\varphi^{\rm MHO}_{\lambda}
by
\chi_{B}\varphi^{\rm MHO}_{\lambda}
, where
\chi_{B}
denotes the indicator to any compact
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, just as long as the resulting vector is not perpendicular to
\varphi_{\lambda}
. Due to the explicit Gaussian form of
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}
, non-orthogonality is guaranteed as long as
B
contains any small disc centered at the origin. In this representation, the resolvent of
h_{\lambda}
acts on a function with compact support. Analyticity of
\varphi_{\lambda}
(up to normalization) then follows from a lemma in which we prove that the mapping

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R}\ni\lambda\mapsto\big(h_{\lambda}-\zeta\mathds{1}\big)^{-1}\chi_{B}\in\mathcal{B}(\chi_{B}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

continues to an analytic function on
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
with exponential upper bounds.

1.2
Consequences and outlook.

As stated in
1.1
, for fixed
b>0
sufficiently small and all
\lambda
outside a set of density zero, we obtain the pointwise lower bounds
eqs.

1.6
and
1.7
and their averages
eqs.

1.9
and
1.10
; the sparsity estimate
eq.

1.8
excludes persistent exact cancellations. Together with
eq.

2.34
, this shows that, on the real axis and away from a discrete set,
\Delta_{0}(\lambda)
and
|\rho_{0}(\lambda)|
are never zero.

We mention some natural questions to consider: (i) Can our smallness assumption on the magnetic parameter,
b
, be relaxed? (ii) Can the exponent in our main theorem:
1+\varepsilon
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, be sharpened to
1
? With such an improvement, could one then extend the work of
[
21
]
on the tight binding reduction in strongly bound and strongly magnetic systems to generic double wells? (iii) What is the precise dependence of the lower bounds of
\Delta_{0}
and
\rho_{0}
on the well-separation parameter,
d_{1}
, and what is the leading term in their asymptotics in the generic case? (iv) Our results concern the splitting of
the lowest two eigenvalues of the magnetic double well Hamiltonian. These eigenvalues perturb from and lie below the first Landau level.
Do analogous results hold for higher Landau levels?
(v) Can our results be extended to non-compactly supported single wells and systems with weak disorder, and three dimensions? Can the regularity assumptions on
v
be relaxed?

1.3
Organization of this paper.

section

2
develops the analytic setup for the hopping coefficient
\rho_{0}
and the splitting
\Delta_{0}
, proves analyticity on wedge-type regions in
\mathbb{C}
, and reduces the double–well problem to a question concerning a
2\times 2
matrix.
section

3
develops a general complex-analytic result, culminating in
3.10
, which facilitates the generic lower bounds of
1.1
.
section

4
deals with the mesoscopic annuli construction used for the parametrix presented in
section

2
. Background on pseudodifferential operators is presented in
appendix

A
, and the partition-of-unity statements are in
appendix

B
. The Landau resolvent for complex magnetic fields and the obstruction to a global analytic continuation are treated in
appendix

C
. Finally an independent appendix, contributed by Tal Shpigel,
appendix

D
establishes resolvent analyticity and off–diagonal decay for the anisotropic magnetic harmonic oscillator needed throughout.

1.4
Notation and conventions.

1.

\chi_{B}
denotes the indicator function of the set
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
\chi_{B}(X)
is the projection operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
onto the subspace of functions supported within
B
.

2.

{\cal B}(X)
is the space of bounded linear operators
X\to X
for a Banach space
X
, and
\mathcal{B}(X\to Y)
denotes the space of bounded linear operators
X\to Y
.

3.

h_{\lambda,b}=h_{\lambda}
, the single-well magnetic Hamiltonian; see
eq.

1.2
.

4.

H_{\lambda,b}=H_{\lambda}
, the double-well magnetic Hamiltonian; see
eq.

1.3
.

5.

\rho_{0}(\lambda)=\rho(\lambda)
, the magnetic hopping coefficient; see
eq.

1.4
.

6.

\Delta_{0}(\lambda)=\Delta(\lambda)=E_{1}(\lambda)-E_{0}(\lambda)
, the magnetic double-well eigenvalue splitting.

7.

If
(x,p)\mapsto a(x,p)
is a smooth symbol, we write
\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)
for the corresponding pseudo-differential
operator.

8.

\mathbb{S}^{1}\equiv\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}}{\left|z\right|=1}
.

9.

Throughout,
\Lambda>0
is a minimal sufficiently large threshold that insures that for any
\left|\lambda\right|\geq\Lambda
our standing hypotheses hold.
\Lambda
is chosen after finitely many constraints and is thus some finite constant dependent on
v
and the constants in our assumptions.
b>0
is an
independent
parameter that encodes the relative strength of the magnetic field compared to the potential well depth. We are generally assuming that
b>0
is less than a small enough constant, but is independent of
\Lambda
(and of
\lambda
of course). In particular, we assume no lower bound on
b>0
.

As such, an expression like
\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)
may well be of order
1
and so may not dominate polynomial factors in
\left|\lambda\right|
. Hence in principle one would have to keep track of all polynomial powers of
\left|\lambda\right|
in addition to exponential expressions, if they include in the exponent a constant proportional to
b
(most of our upper bounds do).

Instead of keeping explicit track of explicit polynomial powers of
\left|\lambda\right|
throughout, we write

\displaystyle A\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)

to indicate

\displaystyle A\leq\left|\lambda\right|^{k}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)

where
k
is a universal constant and
C
depends only on the potential
v
. In different occurrences,
k
and
C
may denote different constants. For instance,

\displaystyle A\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)\land B\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)\Longrightarrow AB\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)\,.

We follow the same convention when estimates involve
\Lambda
instead of
\left|\lambda\right|
.

2
Proof of the main theorem,
1.1

2.1
The analyticity of the hopping coefficient
\lambda\mapsto\rho_{0}(\lambda)

In order to lighten up the notation, from this point forward we shall drop all zero subscripts from objects (which in the introduction denoted an object’s association with
ground states
) unless we need to make a distinction. Thus, we make the replacements
\rho_{0}(\lambda)\mapsto\rho(\lambda),\varphi_{0,\lambda}\mapsto\varphi_{\lambda}
etc. We also suppress the
b
dependence from our notation for all objects.

To prove the main theorem, we shall invoke the complex analytic
3.10
below, whose main inputs, beyond analyticity in a wedge, are a lower bound at a
single point
as well as a global exponential upper bound.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
be given and consider the intermediate region

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}:=\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}}{\Lambda<\left|z\right|<2\Lambda\quad{\rm and}\quad\left|\arg\left(z\right)\right|<\frac{\pi}{2}-\varepsilon}

(2.1)

as well as

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}:=\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}}{\Lambda<\left|z\right|\quad{\rm and}\quad\left|\arg\left(z\right)\right|<\frac{\pi}{2}-\varepsilon}\,.

(2.2)

Our main goal now is to show that the map

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R}\ni\lambda\mapsto\rho(\lambda)\in\mathbb{C}

has an analytic continuation to the domain
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
. Due to the complex conjugation in the expression
eq.

1.4
for
\rho_{0}(\lambda)
, we introduce an equivalent expression which agrees
eq.

1.4
for real
\lambda
and is natural for seeking an analytic extension of
\rho_{0}(\lambda)
to complex
\lambda
:

\displaystyle\rho(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\langle\widehat{R}^{-d}\varphi_{{}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overline{\lambda}}},\left(H_{\lambda}-e_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\widehat{R}^{d}\varphi_{\lambda}\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\overline{\varphi_{{}_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overline{\lambda}}}(x+d)}v(x+d)\exp\left(\operatorname{i}b\lambda d_{1}x_{2}\right)\varphi_{\lambda}(x-d)\operatorname{d}{x}\,.

To prove analyticity of
\rho(\lambda)
on
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
, it clearly suffices to show that the map

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R}\ni\lambda\mapsto\varphi_{\lambda}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

continues analytically to
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

Our general strategy is as follows. Let
\Phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be any trial function which is selected so that it is
not
perpendicular to
\varphi_{\lambda}
and introduce the normalization factor

C_{\lambda}:=\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\Phi\rangle,\quad\lambda>\Lambda.

Let
\xi\in\mathbb{C}\mapsto z_{\lambda}(\xi)
sweep out circle within which
e_{\lambda}
is the only eigenvalue of
h_{\lambda}
and which is of distance
\lambda
from the second eigenvalue of
h_{\lambda}
, uniformly in large
\lambda
; see
eq.

2.8
below. For real and large
\lambda
, we have the following expression for the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})^{2}
normalized ground state of
h_{\lambda}
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C_{\lambda}}\left(\frac{\operatorname{i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}(\xi)\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}z_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{d}{\xi}\right)\Phi\,,

(2.3)

We make no claims about the analytic extension properties of
\lambda\mapsto C_{\lambda}
to
\lambda\notin\mathbb{R}
, although we obtain bounds on it for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and large. We choose
\Phi=\Phi_{\lambda}
so that for large real
\lambda
,

\Phi\approx\varphi_{\lambda}\quad\textrm{in $L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$}\ .

It follows, since the contour integral in
eq.

2.3
is a projection operator, that this contour integral itself is approximately normalized. And since
\varphi_{\lambda}
is normalized,
|C_{\lambda}-1|\ll 1
; a quantitative bound is given in
eq.

2.32
below.

The function

\displaystyle\lambda\mapsto C_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

will be studied as a complex analytic function in
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
leading to a generic lower bound on
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
for
the function

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{C_{\overline{\lambda}}}C_{\lambda}\rho(\lambda)=\lambda^{2}\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\overline{C_{\overline{\lambda}}\varphi_{\overline{\lambda}}(x+d)}v(x+d)\exp\left(\operatorname{i}b\lambda d_{1}x_{2}\right)C_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}(x-d)\operatorname{d}{x}\,

(2.4)

using a result of the type stated in
1.4
; see

section

3
. Concluding a lower bound on
\rho(\lambda)
itself for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\cap\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
outside a set of density zero follows from
C_{\lambda}\approx 1
for real
\lambda
.

The choice of
\mathbb{S}^{1}\ni\xi\mapsto z_{\lambda}(\xi)\in\mathbb{C}
and
\Phi_{\lambda}
.

To proceed, we use the assumption that
v
has a unique non-degenerate minimum, to invoke the results of
[
18
]
.

In particular,

\displaystyle v\left(x\right)=-1+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle x,\left(\nabla\otimes\nabla^{\ast}v\right)(0)x\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}_{x\to 0}\left(\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{3}\right)\,,

where
\left(\nabla\otimes\nabla^{\ast}v\right)
is the Hessian of
v
and we assume
\left(\nabla\otimes\nabla^{\ast}v\right)(0)>0
. As a result, the following model will be of interest to us:

\displaystyle h_{\lambda}^{\text{MHO}}:=\left(P-\frac{1}{2}b\lambda X^{\perp}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\left\langle X,\left(\nabla\otimes\nabla^{\ast}v\right)(0)X\right\rangle\,.

(2.5)

Introduce the dilation
\left(\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}f\right)(x)\equiv\alpha f(\alpha x)
, which is unitary in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
; see also
eq.

A.12
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{U}_{\sqrt{\lambda}}^{\ast}\ h_{\lambda}^{\text{MHO}}\ \mathfrak{U}_{\sqrt{\lambda}}=\lambda h_{1}^{\text{MHO}}:=\lambda h^{\text{MHO}}.

(2.6)

Denote the eigenvalues of
h^{\text{MHO}}
, in ascending order, by
e_{j}^{\text{MHO}}
,
j=0,1,2,\dots
. A consequence of
[
18
]
is that for
\lambda

real
and and
\lambda\geq\Lambda>0
sufficiently large, we have

\displaystyle e_{\lambda,j}=-\lambda^{2}+e_{j}^{\text{MHO}}\lambda+O\left(\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\,,\quad j=0,1.

(2.7)

Below, we may make (finitely many) further restrictions on the positive number
\Lambda
.

For
\lambda\geq\Lambda
, if in
eq.

2.3
we choose
z_{\lambda}(\xi)
as

\displaystyle z_{\lambda}(\xi):=-\lambda^{2}+e_{0}^{\text{MHO}}\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\left(e_{1}^{\text{MHO}}-e_{0}^{\text{MHO}}\right)\xi\lambda\qquad(\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{1})\ ,

(2.8)

then there is exactly one simple eigenvalue of
h_{\lambda}
,
e_{\lambda}
, encircled by our contour, which is at a distance of order
\lambda
from the rest of the spectrum. We note that for fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
\lambda\mapsto z_{\lambda}(\xi)
is entire.

Further, by the approximation of
\varphi_{\lambda}
by
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}

(see
E.1
) we
have that
\|\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\|\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}
and therefore
\|\varphi_{\lambda}-\chi_{B_{a}(0)}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\|\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}
.
Hence, as our trial function in the Riesz projection representation of
\varphi_{\lambda}
,
eq.

2.3
, we shall choose

\Phi_{\lambda}(x):=\chi_{B_{a}(0)}(x)\ \varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}(x)\,.

(2.9)

Rationale for extending the resolvent.

As mentioned in
section

1.1
, a major hurdle arises from

h_{\lambda}
being a relatively compact perturbation of
H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}
, the Landau Hamiltonian
eq.

1.1
, and not of
h_{\lambda}^{\text{MHO}}
.

H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}
is ill-behaved for non-real values of
\lambda
. Indeed, for the Landau gauge, it was shown in
[
15
]
that as soon as
\lambda
gains an imaginary component the spectrum of
H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}
is the whole complex plane. While
[
15
]
does not cover our present case of the symmetric gauge (for complex
\lambda
the two are
not
related by a unitary transformation and hence may not be isospectral) we show below in
C.2
that also in the symmetric gauge, the resolvents of
H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}
and of
h_{\lambda}
do
not
extend to the complex plane.
This is in sharp contrast to the resolvent of
h_{\lambda}^{\text{MHO}}
which does indeed analytically extend with good bounds; we study this below in
D.1
.

Recall that
\operatorname{supp}(v)\subseteq B_{a}(0)
. Let
Q
be the orthogonal projection in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
onto
B_{a}(0)
, i.e.,
Q:=\chi_{B_{a}(0)}(X)
. For the choice
\Phi=\Phi_{\lambda}
given by
eq.

2.9
, the resolvent acts on a function which is supported in
B_{a}(0)
. It is when acting on such compactly supported functions, that we shall define an analytic extension of the resolvent. Specifically, our goal is to define an analytic map

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),

which extends the resolvent acting on functions supported in
B_{a}(0)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle A_{\lambda}\ =\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}Q\quad\textrm{for}\quad\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R},

where
z_{\lambda}=z_{\lambda}(\xi)
is given by
eq.

2.8
. (In place of
Q
, one may use
\chi_{B}(X)
for
any
compact
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, although our particular proof below uses the fact
\operatorname{supp}(v)\subseteq B_{a}(0)
).
A consequence of the construction of
A_{\lambda}
will be that
\mathbb{R}_{\geq\Lambda}\ni\lambda\mapsto C_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
extends analytically to
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

Note that
z_{\lambda}
, and therefore
A_{\lambda}
, depends on
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
; we suppress this dependence, since all statements below hold uniformly for
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

2.1.1
The cut-off Hamiltonian,
h_{\lambda}^{\theta}
, and the analytic extension of its resolvent

As an intermediate step, we consider the Landau Hamiltonian
with a cut-off vector potential
. Let
\theta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,1]
be a smooth cut-off function with compact support such that
\theta=1
on a generous neighborhood (to be specified below) of
B_{a}(0)
, which contains the support of
v
;
{\rm supp}(v)\subset B_{a}(0)\subset\subset{\rm supp}{(\theta)}
. Introduce the cut-off Landau Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{Landau},\theta}_{\lambda}:=\left(P-\frac{1}{2}b\lambda\theta(X)X^{\perp}\right)^{2}\,.

(2.10)

Since
\theta
has compact support,
H^{\mathrm{Landau},\theta}_{\lambda}
is a relatively compact perturbation of the free Laplacian
P^{2}
, and so we expect much better behavior from its resolvent as
\lambda
varies off the real axis into to the complex plane. Further, we introduce
h_{\lambda}^{\theta}
, the one-well Hamiltonian with cut-off vector potential:

\displaystyle h_{\lambda}^{\theta}:=H^{\mathrm{Landau},\theta}_{\lambda}+\lambda^{2}v(X)\,.

(2.11)

We begin by extending the resolvent of
h_{\lambda}^{\theta}
, initially defined for real coupling constant,
\lambda
, to a well-defined operator for
\lambda
varying in a complex region
\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}
; see
eq.

2.1
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,\pi/2)
and
\Lambda
sufficiently large.
The map

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R}\ni\lambda\mapsto\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

extends to a map

\displaystyle A^{\theta}_{\lambda}:\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

which satisfies the following properties:

1.

\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)A^{\theta}_{\lambda}=\mathds{1}
for all
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

2.

\lambda\mapsto A_{\lambda}^{\theta}
is analytic on
\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

3.

For some
\eta>0
, sufficiently small and independent of
\Lambda
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\lVert A_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right\rVert\lesssim\Lambda^{-\eta}\,,

(2.12)

for all
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

Proof of
2.1
.

Let us introduce a partition of unity

\displaystyle\Set{\chi_{\nu}}_{\nu=0}^{N_{\Lambda}}\,

(2.13)

where
N_{\Lambda}<\infty
is to be specified below. We pick the partition as follows. For any
\nu\in\Set{0,\cdots,N_{\Lambda}}
,
\chi_{\nu}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,1])
and
1=\sum_{\nu=0}^{N_{\Lambda}}\chi_{\nu}
. We divide the members of the partition into three categories:

1.

\nu=0
:
\chi_{0}
is centered at the origin and has support of diameter of order
\delta\to 0
as
\Lambda\to\infty
, for some
\delta
to be chosen below. To be concrete, we choose
\chi_{0}
to equal
1
on
B_{C\delta}(0)
for some sufficiently large order
1
constant
C
.

2.

\nu=N_{\Lambda}
:
\chi_{N_{\Lambda}}
is supported in the complement of a sufficiently large disc of radius
R
of order
1
(in
\Lambda
) chosen so that the disc entirely covers
\operatorname{supp}(\theta)
.

3.

\nu=1,\cdots,N_{\Lambda}-1
:
\chi_{\nu}
is supported in an annulus with radii between
2^{\nu-1}\delta
to
2^{\nu+1}\delta
. Hence we choose

\displaystyle N_{\Lambda}:=\lceil\log_{2}(R/\delta)\rceil\ .

(2.14)

The construction of this partition is presented below in
appendix

B
.

In addition to
\Set{\chi_{\nu}}_{\nu}
, we introduce a slightly more generous collection
\Set{\psi_{\nu}}_{\nu}
such that
\psi_{\nu}=1
on
\operatorname{supp}(\chi_{\nu})
and such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, no more than, say,
four
values of
\nu
have
x\in\operatorname{supp}(\psi_{\nu})
. We shall also make use of open subsets
U_{\nu}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
below, each defined so it contains the support of
\psi_{\nu}
. We shall take

\displaystyle U_{\nu}\subseteq\Set{2^{\nu-3}\delta<\left\lVert x\right\rVert<2^{\nu+3}\delta}\,.

We then write a putative expression for the analytic continuation of
\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{A^{\theta}_{\lambda}}:=\psi_{0}\left(h_{\lambda}^{\text{MHO}}-\left(z_{\lambda}+\lambda^{2}\right)\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}\chi_{0}+\sum_{\nu=1}^{N_{\Lambda}-1}T_{\lambda,\nu}+\psi_{N_{\Lambda}}\left(P^{2}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}\chi_{N_{\Lambda}}\,.

The operators
T_{\lambda,\nu}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
should roughly be considered as being given by

\displaystyle T_{\lambda,\nu}"="\psi_{\nu}\left(h^{\theta}_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}\chi_{\nu}\,,

where
z_{\lambda}=z_{\lambda}(\xi)
is given
eq.

2.8
. The issue is that we do not know a-priori that
h^{\theta}_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}
is invertible for
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
(indeed, that’s precisely what we’re trying to prove) so we cannot write down such an expression, even if it’s restricted between
\chi_{\nu}
and
\psi_{\nu}
. To deal with this problem, we use the formalism of pseudo-differential operators (see
appendix

A
below). Using this formalism,
T_{\lambda,\nu}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is constructed as follows. We work with the symbol
s_{\lambda}^{\theta}
associated with
h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}
, but consider it as a symbol where the position variable ranges over
U_{\nu}
rather than the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. If we can show ellipticity of the operator associated with this symbol, general pseudo-differential arguments (
A.5
below, applied to a rescaling of
s_{\lambda}^{\theta}
) guarantee the existence of an approximate analytic inverse
T_{\lambda,\nu}
with appropriate bounds, in particular, we will derive

\displaystyle\left\lVert T_{\lambda,\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U_{\nu}))}\lesssim\frac{1}{\Lambda^{2}2^{2\nu}\delta^{2}}

(2.16)

and

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)T_{\lambda,\nu}-\chi_{\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U_{\nu}))}\lesssim\frac{1}{\Lambda 2^{2\nu}\delta^{2}}\,.

(2.17)

This prompts us to choose
\delta
so that

\displaystyle\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\Lambda\delta^{2}=\infty\,.

(2.18)

Indeed, we take

\displaystyle\delta=\Lambda^{\eta-1/2}

(2.19)

for some small
\eta>0
.
Let

r_{\lambda,\nu}(z_{\lambda}):=\begin{cases}\left(h_{\lambda}^{\text{MHO}}-\left(z_{\lambda}+\lambda^{2}\right)\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}&\nu=0\\
T_{\lambda,\nu}&\nu\in\Set{1,\cdots,N_{\Lambda}-1}\\
\left(P^{2}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}&\nu=N_{\Lambda}\end{cases}

We calculate
\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\widetilde{A^{\theta}_{\lambda}}
,
where
\widetilde{A^{\theta}_{\lambda}}
is given by
section

2.1.1
:

\displaystyle\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\widetilde{A^{\theta}_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu=0,N_{\Lambda}}\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\psi_{\nu}r_{\lambda,\nu}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{\nu}+\sum_{\nu=1,\cdots,N_{\Lambda}-1}\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)T_{\lambda,\nu}

\displaystyle=:\mathds{1}+K_{\lambda}^{\theta}\ .

The error term is explicitly given by:

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}^{\theta}

\displaystyle:=\left[h_{\lambda}^{\theta},\psi_{0}\right]r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{0}+\psi_{0}\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}+\lambda^{2}\mathds{1}-h_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}\right)r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{0}+

(2.20)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\nu=1}^{N_{\Lambda}-1}\left(\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)T_{\lambda,\nu}-\chi_{\nu}\right)+\left[h_{\lambda}^{\theta},\psi_{N_{\Lambda}}\right]\left(P^{2}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}\chi_{N_{\Lambda}}

We will show that
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto K_{\lambda}^{\theta}
is analytic and moreover, with the choice
eq.

2.19
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert K_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim N_{\lambda}\Lambda^{-\eta}\sim\log\left(\Lambda\right)\Lambda^{-\eta}

so that the Neumann series may be inverted to yield the sought after analytic inverse

\displaystyle\boxed{A_{\lambda}^{\theta}:=\widetilde{A^{\theta}_{\lambda}}\left(\mathds{1}+K_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right)^{-1}}\,.

We note that it suffices to bound the norm of each summand within
K_{\lambda}
separately since there are only
N_{\Lambda}\sim\log(\Lambda)
terms, each of which decays at least like
\Lambda^{-\eta}
.

The
\nu=0
term: the MHO.

We deal with the two terms in
eq.

2.20
separately. For the commutator,

\displaystyle\left[h_{\lambda}^{\theta},\psi_{0}\right]r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{0}=\left[H_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{Landau},\theta},\psi_{0}\right]r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{0}\,.

Since by
eq.

2.10
, we have
H_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{Landau},\theta}\equiv\left(P-\frac{1}{2}b\lambda\theta(X)X^{\perp}\right)^{2}
we learn that

\displaystyle\left[H_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{Landau},\theta},\psi_{0}\right]=\left[P^{2},\psi_{0}\right]-b\lambda\theta(X)X\wedge\left[P,\psi_{0}\right]=-\left(\Delta\psi_{0}\right)-2\operatorname{i}\left(\nabla\psi_{0}\right)\cdot P+\operatorname{i}b\lambda\theta(X)X\wedge\left(\nabla\psi_{0}\right)\,.

Now, thanks to
D.1
, all terms are indeed analytic in
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
. Moreover, since
\psi_{0}
is equal to
1
on a somewhat enlarged neighborhood containing
\operatorname{supp}(\chi_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(\operatorname{supp}(\chi_{0}),\operatorname{supp}(\partial_{i}\psi_{0}))\gtrsim c\delta\,.

But now we invoke the estimate proven in
D.1

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{S}(X)P_{j}^{\alpha}r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{T}(X)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\exp\left(-c\Lambda\mathrm{dist}(S,T)^{2}\right)\qquad(\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\text{ and }S,T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle j=1,2,\alpha=0,1)

which holds as soon as
\mathrm{dist}(S,T)>\Lambda^{-1/2}
.

Thanks to
eq.

2.19
we may conclude
\left\lVert\left[h_{\lambda}^{\theta},\psi_{0}\right]r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{0}\right\rVert\lesssim\exp\left(-c\Lambda^{\eta}\right)=o(1)
.

For the second term,

\displaystyle\psi_{0}\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}+\lambda^{2}\mathds{1}-h_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}\right)r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{0}

we have, using the fact that
\theta=1
on
\operatorname{supp}(\psi_{0})
,

\displaystyle\psi_{0}\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}+\lambda^{2}\mathds{1}-h_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}\right)

\displaystyle=\psi_{0}\left(\lambda^{2}\left(v(X)+1\right)-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\langle X,\left(\mathbb{H}v\right)(0)X\rangle\right)\,.

We recall
\delta\equiv\delta_{\Lambda}
was the support radius of
\psi_{0}
. Below in
D.1
we prove the estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\Lambda^{-1}

(2.22)

so that all together we estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert\psi_{0}\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-h_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}\right)r_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{MHO}}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{0}\right\rVert\lesssim\Lambda\delta^{3}\,.

The bound depends also on the third derivatives of
v
.

We see that if we pick
\delta
smaller than
\Lambda^{-1/3}
we can make this term arbitrarily small; this is compatible with
eq.

2.19
.

The term
\nu=N_{\Lambda}
: the free Laplacian.

Here the support of
\chi_{\nu}
has no intersection with
\operatorname{supp}(\theta)
. Since
\operatorname{supp}(\theta)\supseteq\operatorname{supp}(v)
, this means that in that region, our operator agrees with the free Laplacian
P^{2}
, which is why we approximate its resolvent by
\left(P^{2}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}
.

We shall use freely the trivial estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(P^{2}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\inf_{E\in[0,\infty)}|E-z_{\lambda}|}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sin(\varepsilon)\Lambda^{2}}\,.

(2.23)

Moreover, we also have the Combes-Thomas estimate: For any
S,T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
j=1,2
and
\alpha=0,1
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{S}P_{j}^{\alpha}\left(P^{2}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}\chi_{T}\right\rVert\lesssim\exp\left(-\sqrt{\sin(\varepsilon)\Lambda^{2}}\mathrm{dist}(S,T)\right)\,.

(2.24)

As a result, we see the commutator term

\displaystyle\left[h_{\lambda}^{\theta},\psi_{N_{\Lambda}}\right]\left(P^{2}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}\chi_{N_{\Lambda}}

(2.25)

has exponentially decaying norm. The distance between
\operatorname{supp}(\left[h_{\lambda}^{\theta},\psi_{N_{\Lambda}}\right])
and
\operatorname{supp}(\chi_{N_{\Lambda}})
is of order
1
.

The terms
\nu=1,\cdots,N_{\Lambda}-1
.

We postpone the construction of
T_{\lambda,\nu}
which obey the estimates
eqs.

2.16
and
2.17
to
section

4
below.

The proof of
2.1
is now complete.∎

Below we will also need the following

Lemma 2.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{j}^{\alpha}A_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right\rVert\lesssim\Lambda^{-1/2+\eta}\qquad(\alpha=0,1;j=1,2)

(2.26)

Proof.

If
\alpha=0
the claim is covered by the analysis above (collecting all the various estimates on the terms in
\widetilde{A_{\lambda}^{\theta}}
yields
\left\lVert A_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right\rVert\lesssim\Lambda^{-1}
).

Next, thanks to

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{j}A_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert P_{j}\widetilde{A_{\lambda}^{\theta}}\left(\mathds{1}+K_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert P_{j}\widetilde{A_{\lambda}^{\theta}}\right\rVert\left\lVert\left(\mathds{1}+K_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert

it suffices to bound
P_{j}
acting on each individual term in
section

2.1.1
. To that end, we proceed in much the same fashion as we have above.

For
\nu=0
or
\nu=N_{\Lambda}
, we first take care of the commutator term
[P_{j},\psi_{\nu}]r_{\lambda,\nu}(z_{\lambda})\chi_{\nu}
, which boils down to the boundedness of the resolvent of either the MHO or the free Laplacian. Next,
P_{j}r_{\lambda,\nu}(z_{\lambda})
is also bounded: for the MHO this is proven below in
D.1
and for the free Laplacian it is standard.

Finally for
\nu=1,\cdots,N_{\Lambda-1}
,
A.5
below yields also the estimates

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{j}T_{\lambda,\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U_{\nu}))}\lesssim\frac{1}{\Lambda^{2}2^{2\nu}\delta^{3}}

and hence the result.
∎

2.1.2
Removing the cut-off to get from
h_{\lambda}^{\theta}
to
h_{\lambda}

As we explained, we cannot hope to analytically continue
\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}
as an operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. So we merely try to construct an analytic extension of
\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}Q
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The one-well resolvent

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq\Lambda}\ni\lambda\mapsto\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}Q\in\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

extends to a function

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

such that

1.

\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)A_{\lambda}=Q
for all
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

2.

\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is analytic.

3.

We have the bound

\displaystyle\left\lVert A_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)\,,

(2.27)

for all
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

4.

A_{\lambda}
and its derivatives exhibit off-diagonal exponential decay in the sense that, for all
j=1,2
and
\alpha=0,1
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{S}P_{j}^{\alpha}A_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\exp\left(-c\left|\lambda\right|\mathrm{dist}(S,B_{2a}(0))\right)\qquad(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2})\,.

Proof of
2.3
.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,1])
be some cut-off function to be determined below. We assume
\chi=1
on
B_{a}(0)
, so
\chi Q=Q
, and moreover, that
\theta=1
on
\operatorname{supp}(\chi)
.

We begin by calculating

\displaystyle\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\chi A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q

\displaystyle=\left[\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right),\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q+\chi\left(h_{\lambda}-h_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right)A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q+Q

\displaystyle=\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q+Q\,.

Now we would like to argue that the first term is small so that we can find a right inverse on
QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. However, the problem is the commutator
\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]
"leaks" outside of
Q
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q=Q\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q+Q^{\perp}\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q

and
Q^{\perp}\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q\neq 0
.

To deal with this issue, we apply the Landau resolvent on the left and rewrite it as

\displaystyle\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q

\displaystyle=\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)R^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q-\lambda^{2}v(X)R^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q\,.

This makes sense, even though
\lambda
may not be real, thanks to
C.1
down below, which yields an extension of
R^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})
when acting on functions with (fixed) compact support;
\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]
is an operator of the form
f(X)+g(X)P
where both
f,g
have compact support within
\operatorname{supp}(\partial_{j}\chi)
.

Re-arranging and using the fact that
Qv(X)=v(X)
we find

\displaystyle\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\widetilde{A_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=Q+K_{\lambda}

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{A_{\lambda}}:=\left(\chi-R^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]\right)A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q

and

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}:=-\lambda^{2}Qv(X)R^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q\,.

We remark that
K_{\lambda}
is now an operator
QL^{2}\to QL^{2}
whereas
A_{\lambda}:QL^{2}\to L^{2}
.

Hence if we can show that
\left\lVert K_{\lambda}\right\rVert<1
we would be finished via

\displaystyle\boxed{A_{\lambda}:=\widetilde{A_{\lambda}}\left(\mathds{1}+K_{\lambda}\right)^{-1}}\,.

To do so we will use two basic facts:
eq.

2.26
and
eq.

C.7
. The latter, which is applicable thanks to our small
b
assumption, means we have exponential decay in
\Lambda
since
\mathrm{dist}(\operatorname{supp}(v),\partial_{j}\chi)\gtrsim O(1)
, but the former means there can’t be any term in
P_{j}A_{\lambda}^{\theta}
which explodes faster (in fact the norm of this term decays too). Hence
\left\lVert K_{\lambda}\right\rVert
is indeed arbitrarily small for large
\lambda
.

Finally, we tend to establishing
item

4
. Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be such that
\mathrm{dist}(S,\operatorname{supp}(\chi))>0
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{S}P_{j}^{\alpha}A_{\lambda}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\chi_{S}P_{j}^{\alpha}\left(\chi-R^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]\right)A_{\lambda}^{\theta}Q\left(\mathds{1}+K_{\lambda}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert

(2.29)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\chi_{S}P_{j}^{\alpha}R^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})\left[h_{\lambda},\chi\right]\right\rVert\left\lVert A_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right\rVert\frac{1}{1-\left\lVert K_{\lambda}\right\rVert}

(2.30)

and we then invoke
eq.

C.7
again. We now choose
\chi
to have support within
B_{2a}(0)
to get the statement.

Intermediate conclusion.

So far we managed to define an operator
A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
such that

1.

\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)A_{\lambda}=Q
for all
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

2.

\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is analytic.

3.

We have the bound
\left\lVert A_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\Lambda^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\Lambda\right)
for some
C\in\mathbb{R}
, for all
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

4.

We have off-diagonal exponential decay in the sense that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{S}A_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\exp\left(-c\left|\lambda\right|\mathrm{dist}(S,B_{2a}(0))\right)\qquad(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2})

for some
c>0
, for all
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,2\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

Patching different regions in
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

Fix a number
r\in(1,2)
and set

\Lambda_{k}:=r^{k}\Lambda,\qquad\mathcal{D}_{k}:=\Omega_{\Lambda_{k},2\Lambda_{k},\varepsilon}=\bigl\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Lambda_{k}<|z|<2\Lambda_{k},\ \ |\arg z|<\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\varepsilon\bigr\}.

Then
\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{D}_{k}=\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
and consecutive domains have a nontrivial overlap:

\mathcal{D}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k+1}=\bigl\{z:\Lambda_{k+1}<|z|<2\Lambda_{k},\ \ |\arg z|<\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\varepsilon\bigr\}\neq\varnothing

because
r<2
.

For each
k
the construction above produces an analytic map

\mathcal{D}_{k}\ni\lambda\mapsto A^{(k)}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

and satisfies

\bigl(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\bigr)A^{(k)}_{\lambda}=Q\qquad(\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{k}),

and obeys
\|A^{(k)}_{\lambda}\|\lesssim\Lambda_{k}^{\sharp}\exp(Cb\Lambda_{k})
as well as
\left\lVert\chi_{S}A_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\exp\left(-c\Lambda_{k}\mathrm{dist}(S,B_{2a}(0))\right)
(all bounds uniform in
\xi\in\partial B_{1}(0)
).

Fix
k
and consider
\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k+1}
with
\lambda>0
real. Then by construction
z_{\lambda}(\xi)\in\rho(h_{\lambda})
(the resolvent set) for all
\xi\in\partial B_{1}(0)
; hence
\bigl(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\bigr)^{-1}
exists as a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Since both
A^{(k)}_{\lambda}
and
A^{(k+1)}_{\lambda}
are
right
inverses on
QL^{2}
,

\bigl(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\bigr)A^{(k)}_{\lambda}=\bigl(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\bigr)A^{(k+1)}_{\lambda}=Q,

and the true inverse on
L^{2}
is unique, we must have

A^{(k)}_{\lambda}=\bigl(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\bigr)^{-1}Q=A^{(k+1)}_{\lambda}\qquad(\lambda\in(\Lambda_{k+1},2\Lambda_{k})\subset\mathbb{R}).

Thus the two analytic
\mathcal{B}(QL^{2})
-valued maps
A^{(k)}
and
A^{(k+1)}
agree on the real interval
(\Lambda_{k+1},2\Lambda_{k})
, which has accumulation points in the overlap
\mathcal{D}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k+1}
. By the identity theorem for Banach–valued holomorphic functions, they coincide on all of
\mathcal{D}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k+1}
.

Define
A_{\lambda}
on
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
by choosing any
k
with
\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
and setting
A_{\lambda}:=A^{(k)}_{\lambda}
. The overlap consistency shows this is well defined. The resulting map

\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\longmapsto A_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

is analytic, and for
\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
the bounds

\|A_{\lambda}\|=\|A^{(k)}_{\lambda}\|\ \lesssim\Lambda_{k}^{\sharp}\ \exp\!\bigl(Cb\,\Lambda_{k}\bigr)\ \leq\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\ \exp\!\bigl(Cb\,|\lambda|\bigr)

as well as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{S}A_{\lambda}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\chi_{S}A^{k}_{\lambda}\right\rVert\lesssim\exp\left(-c\Lambda_{k}\mathrm{dist}(S,B_{2a}(0))\right)\leq\exp\left(-\tilde{c}\left|\lambda\right|\mathrm{dist}(S,B_{2a}(0))\right)

hold because
|\lambda|\in(\Lambda_{k},2\Lambda_{k})
.

If one repeats the construction on
\mathcal{D}_{k}
with different auxiliary data
(\theta,\chi,\delta,\{\chi_{\nu}\},\{\psi_{\nu}\})
, the two resulting analytic right inverses agree for all real
\lambda\in(\Lambda_{k},2\Lambda_{k})
(both equal
(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1})^{-1}Q
), hence agree on
\mathcal{D}_{k}
by the identity theorem. Therefore the global
A_{\lambda}
is canonical and independent of these choices.

The proof of
2.3
is now complete.
∎

2.2
From the analyticity of
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto C_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}
to a lower bound on
\rho_{0}
.

We next tie together the previous results in order to apply
3.10
.
So far we have established that

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto\overline{C_{\overline{\lambda}}}C_{\lambda}\rho(\lambda)\in\mathbb{C}

is analytic.

To apply
3.10
we need lower bound on
\left|\overline{C_{\overline{\lambda}}}C_{\lambda}\rho(\lambda)\right|
at a single point of
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
and global upper bounds on all
\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

The global upper bound.

For the upper bound, we begin by estimating

\displaystyle\left|\overline{C_{\overline{\lambda}}}C_{\lambda}\rho(\lambda)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\left|\lambda\right|^{4}\int_{x\in B_{a}(0)}\exp\left(b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|d_{1}x_{2}\right)\left\lVert A_{\lambda}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\Phi\right\rVert^{2}\operatorname{d}{x}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)\,.

Moreover, recalling the exact ground state of the magnetic harmonic oscillator is of the form

\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}(x):=C\sqrt{\lambda}\exp\left(-\lambda\langle x,Sx\rangle\right)\qquad(x\in\mathbb{R}^{2})

(2.31)

for some
2\times 2
matrix
S>0
, and constant
C>0
, both dependent on the Hessian of
v
at the origin, we take

\Phi
of the form

\displaystyle\Phi=Q\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\,.

In particular,
\left\lVert\Phi\right\rVert^{2}-1=\left\lVert Q^{\perp}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\right\rVert^{2}\approx\exp\left(-c\left|\lambda\right|a^{2}\right)
.

Now that we’ve finally chosen
\Phi
we can bound
C_{\lambda}

for real
\lambda
. Indeed, by
E.1
below, if
P_{\lambda}:=\varphi_{\lambda}\otimes\varphi_{\lambda}^{\ast}
, then, with
P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\equiv\mathds{1}-P_{\lambda}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\qquad(\lambda\in\mathbb{R})\,.

By definition, we have
C_{\lambda}=\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\Phi\rangle
so that

\displaystyle\left|\left|C_{\lambda}\right|^{2}-1\right|=1-\left|\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\Phi\rangle\right|^{2}=1-\left\lVert\Phi\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\Phi\right\rVert^{2}\approx\exp\left(-c\lambda a^{2}\right)+\left\lVert P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\Phi\right\rVert^{2}

and
\left\lVert P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\Phi\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert P_{\lambda}^{\perp}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\right\rVert+\left\lVert P_{\lambda}^{\perp}Q^{\perp}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\right\rVert\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}
so we learn

\displaystyle\left|\left|C_{\lambda}\right|^{2}-1\right|\lesssim\lambda^{-1}\qquad(\lambda\in\mathbb{R})\,.

(2.32)

The lower bound at a point.

In this section we shall take
b
sufficiently small, and hence make explicit the dependence of
\rho_{0}
on
b
as well; we write

\rho_{0}(\lambda,b)
.

For
b=0
, let
\lambda_{\star}\geq 2\Lambda
be some value of such that

\displaystyle\left|\rho_{0}(\lambda_{\star},b=0)\right|\geq C\lambda_{\star}^{2}\exp\left(-2cd_{1}\lambda_{\star}\right)

(2.33)

which follows, for example, from the analysis in
[
4
, Section 15.3]
. Without any quantitative bounds on
b_{\star}
, we know that there exists
some

b_{\star}>0
such that if
b\in(0,b_{\star})
then

\displaystyle\left|\rho_{0}(\lambda_{\star},b)\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\left|\rho_{0}(\lambda_{\star},b=0)\right|

In particular this also means that for such
b
,

\displaystyle\left|C_{\lambda_{\star}}\right|^{2}\left|\rho_{0}(\lambda_{\star},b)\right|\gtrsim\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{C}{\lambda_{\star}}\right)C\lambda_{\star}^{2}\exp\left(-2cd_{1}\lambda_{\star}\right)\,.

Invoking
3.10
.

We are now prepared to invoke our main complex analytic result. We use the replacement

\displaystyle\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}:-\alpha\pi/2<\operatorname{Arg}(z)<\alpha\pi/2}\ni z\mapsto\left(\lambda_{\star}-\Lambda\right)z+\Lambda=:\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}

with
\alpha\equiv 1-\frac{2\varepsilon}{\pi}
. In terms of this replacement, our lower bound at
z=1
is

\displaystyle\left|C\right|^{2}\left|\rho_{0}\right|\geq\exp\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\lambda_{\star}}\right)C\lambda_{\star}^{2}\right)-2cd_{1}\lambda_{\star}\right)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\beta:=2cd_{1}\lambda_{\star}-\log\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\lambda_{\star}}\right)C\lambda_{\star}^{2}\right)\,.

The global upper bound is

\displaystyle\left|C\right|^{2}\left|\rho_{0}\right|\leq\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)=\left|\left(\lambda_{\star}-\Lambda\right)z+\Lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\left(\lambda_{\star}-\Lambda\right)z+\Lambda\right|\right)=:\left|U(z)\right|\,.

3.10
thus yields,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|C_{(\lambda_{\star}-\Lambda)t+\Lambda}\right|^{2}\left|\rho_{0}((\lambda_{\star}-\Lambda)t+\Lambda)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha 2^{1/\alpha}\left(\beta+\log\left(\left|U(1)\right|\right)\right)R^{1/\alpha}+\frac{3}{2}cbR\,.

Now we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|C_{(\lambda_{\star}-\Lambda)t+\Lambda}\right|^{2}\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(1-\frac{1}{(\lambda_{\star}-\Lambda)t+\Lambda}\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\lesssim\frac{1}{R}\,.

Hence, the highest order upper bound on
\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|\rho_{0}((\lambda_{\star}-\Lambda)t+\Lambda)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}
is indeed the one claimed in
eq.

1.9
.

2.3
The analyticity of the splitting
\lambda\mapsto\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}\Delta_{0}(\lambda)^{2}

Motivation for the proof.

For real
\lambda
, if we have good lower bounds on
\rho_{0}(\lambda)
(see below) then we may prove

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\frac{\Delta_{0}(\lambda)}{2\left|\rho_{0}(\lambda)\right|}=1

(2.34)

(see e.g.
[
5
]
). Hence one could have hoped that for such
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
at which we have established
eq.

1.6
we could pass directly to
eq.

1.7
via
eq.

2.34
. Unfortunately this is doomed to fail since in the proof of
eq.

2.34
a lower bound of the form

\displaystyle\left|\rho_{0}(\lambda)\right|\geq\lambda^{-\sharp}\exp\left(-c\lambda d_{1}^{2}\right)

is necessary and we have something markedly worse; this lower bound then has to compete with error terms which have upper bounds of the form

\displaystyle\left|\rm{error}\right|\leq\lambda^{\sharp}\exp\left(-c\lambda d_{2}^{2}\right)

with
d_{2}>d_{1}
which allows one to proceed. But now we have
\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}
competing with
\lambda
so that argument is doomed to fail.

Moreover, there is also the problem that even if we succeeded in doing that, we might have obtained a lower bound on
\Delta_{0}
, but we wouldn’t have obtained
eq.

1.8
.

Instead, we show the analyticity of
\Delta_{0}
directly, applying the complex analytic lemma to obtain an average lower bound.

Lemma 2.4
.

There exists a function
\lambda\mapsto\gamma_{\lambda}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq\Lambda}\ni\lambda\mapsto\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}\Delta_{0}(\lambda)^{2}

extends to a function

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto\Sigma_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}

1.

\lambda\mapsto\Sigma_{\lambda}
is analytic.

2.

We have the bound
\left|\Sigma_{\lambda}\right|\lesssim\exp\left(c\left|\lambda\right|b\right)
.

3.

For real
\lambda
,

\displaystyle\left|1-\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}\right|\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\,.

This lemma now yields the claimed lower bound in
eq.

1.10
. Indeed, given the lemma above the rest of the proof proceeds identically as above for
\rho
, and in particular, for
b
sufficiently small, there exists some
\lambda_{\star}
so that there is a lower bound on
\Delta_{0}(\lambda_{\star})
via the lower bound on
\rho_{0}(\lambda_{\star})
and the usual connection between
\rho_{0}
and
\Delta_{0}
for real
\lambda
, i.e.,
eq.

2.34
.

Proof outline for
2.4
.

We proceed in the following steps:

1.

For the same reasons explained above, we cannot hope to analytically continue the double-well resolvent

\displaystyle\lambda\mapsto R_{\lambda}(z)\equiv\left(\left(P-\frac{1}{2}b\lambda X^{\perp}\right)^{2}+\lambda^{2}v(X+d)+\lambda^{2}v(X-d)-z\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}

to
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
. Instead, we let
Q_{\pm d}:=\chi_{B_{a}(\pm d)}(X)
and
Q_{-d,d}:=Q_{-d}+Q_{d}
(also a projection); we shall analytically continue

\displaystyle R_{\lambda}(z)Q_{-d,d}\,.

2.

To do so, we cannot directly use the proof above, but a perturbative argument shall suffice to pass from
A_{\lambda}=\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}Q
to the existence of
R_{\lambda}(z)Q_{-d,d}
.

3.

For real
\lambda
, let
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the two-dimensional eigenspace of
H_{\lambda}
associated with its two lowest eigenvalues if they are each simple, or its lowest eigenvalue if it is doubly degenerate. The results of Matsumoto
[
18
]
establish that in the vicinity of
eq.

2.8
, there are exactly two eigenvalues for
H_{\lambda}
when
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and sufficiently large. As a result, using
R_{\lambda}(z)Q_{-d,d}
, at real values of
\lambda
, a contour integral with
z_{\lambda}
as in
eq.

2.8
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\lambda}Q_{-d,d}=\frac{\operatorname{i}}{2\pi}\oint_{\xi\in\partial B_{1}(0)}R_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})Q_{-d,d}z_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{d}{\xi}

yields the spectral projection
\Pi_{\lambda}
onto
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
after projection with
Q_{-d,d}
.

If we now apply this operator to two trial functions, call them
\Phi_{\pm d}
, which are assumed to be in the range of
Q_{\pm d}
respectively to get
\psi_{\pm_{d}}:=\Pi_{\lambda}\Phi_{\pm d}
then, if we can establish that
\psi_{\pm d}
are non-zero and linearly independent, we would find that

\displaystyle\operatorname{span}\left(\Set{\psi_{-d},\psi_{d}}\right)=\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}\,.

(2.36)

We note that our choices will not yield
\Set{\psi_{-d},\psi_{d}}
as an orthonormal set but it will nearly be so for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. The maps

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto\psi_{\pm d}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(2.37)

are manifestly analytic, but if we orthonormalized the set we might spoil that property.

4.

We consider now the
2\times 2
matrices

\displaystyle M_{\lambda}:=\Set{\langle\psi_{\alpha,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overline{\lambda}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},H_{\lambda}\psi_{\beta,\lambda}\rangle}_{\alpha,\beta=\pm d}\qquad G_{\lambda}:=\Set{\langle\psi_{\alpha,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overline{\lambda}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\psi_{\beta,\lambda}\rangle}_{\alpha,\beta=\pm d}\,.

(2.38)

These two matrices clearly have elements which are analytic functions of
\lambda
by the considerations above.

Routine linear algebra (see below) shows that, when
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, with
E_{1}(\lambda),E_{0}(\lambda)
the two eigenvalues of
H_{\lambda}
within
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
,

\displaystyle\det(G_{\lambda})^{2}\left(E_{1}(\lambda)-E_{0}(\lambda)\right)^{2}=\operatorname{tr}\left(\operatorname{adj}(G_{\lambda})M_{\lambda}\right)^{2}-4\det(G_{\lambda})\det(M_{\lambda})

with

\displaystyle\operatorname{adj}(M)\equiv\begin{bmatrix}m_{22}&-m_{12}\\
-m_{21}&m_{11}\end{bmatrix}\qquad(M\in\operatorname{Mat}_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{C}))\,.

(2.40)

I.e., we find

\displaystyle\det(G_{\lambda})^{2}\Delta_{0}(\lambda)^{2}=\operatorname{tr}\left(\operatorname{adj}(G_{\lambda})M_{\lambda}\right)^{2}-4\det(G_{\lambda})\det(M_{\lambda})\qquad(\lambda\in\mathbb{R})

and so clearly the map

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq\Lambda}\ni\lambda\mapsto\det(G_{\lambda})^{2}\Delta_{0}(\lambda)^{2}\in\mathbb{C}

(2.42)

continues to an analytic function

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\Sigma_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}

(2.43)

via the RHS of
item

4
. Bounding the matrix elements yields an upper bound on the splitting.

2.3.1
Construction of
R_{\lambda}(z)Q_{-d,d}

The argument above for analytically continuing
\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}Q
relied on the origin being centered at the minimum of
v
, where it is quadratic. To deal with the double-well system, we would have to adapt that argument to allow for two centers.

Lemma 2.5
.

For the spectral parameter
z_{\lambda}
as in
eq.

2.8
, if the resolvent of the double-well Hamiltonian is given in
item

1
, then
\mathbb{R}\ni\lambda\mapsto R_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})Q_{-d,d}
extends to an analytic function

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto A_{\lambda}^{-d,d}\in\mathcal{B}(Q_{-d,d}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

with the bound

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{j}^{\alpha}A_{\lambda}^{-d,d}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(Q_{-d,d}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)\qquad(j=1,2;\alpha=0,1,2)\,.

(2.44)

Proof.

We shall use the magnetic translation operators given in
eq.

1.5
, here factorized as

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{z}=\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}z\cdot P\right)\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}\frac{1}{2}b\lambda z\cdot X^{\perp}\right)\qquad(z\in\mathbb{R}^{2})\,.

(2.45)

These operators are unitary if
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and otherwise generally not even bounded; in factorized form the two exponentials commute.
\widehat{R}^{z}
commutes with the magnetic kinetic energy
H^{\rm Landau}_{b,\lambda}
and obeys

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\pm d}v(X)\widehat{R}^{\mp d}=v(X\mp d)

(2.46)

so we have the relation

\displaystyle\widehat{R}^{\pm d}\left(H^{\rm Landau}_{b,\lambda}+\lambda^{2}v(X)\right)\widehat{R}^{\mp d}=H^{\rm Landau}_{b,\lambda}+\lambda^{2}v(X\mp d)\,.

(2.47)

Consequently, via the analytic extension of
\left(h_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}Q
,
A_{\lambda}
, constructed above, we now have right inverse of the translates of the one-well Hamiltonians too:

\displaystyle\left(H^{\rm Landau}_{b,\lambda}+\lambda^{2}v(X\mp d)-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\widehat{R}^{\pm d}A_{\lambda}\widehat{R}^{\mp d}=Q_{\pm d}\,.

It is clear that the map

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\ni\lambda\mapsto A_{\lambda}^{\pm d}:=\widehat{R}^{\pm d}A_{\lambda}\widehat{R}^{\mp d}\in\mathcal{B}(Q_{\pm d}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

is also analytic, but if
\lambda
is not real, due to the magnetic translations not being unitary, we have a deteriorated bound. First note that in the magnetic translations, for complex
\lambda
,
\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}z\cdot P\right)
is still unitary and commutes with the other term, so it becomes irrelevant in the operator norm. We thus only keep the factor
\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}\frac{1}{2}b\lambda z\cdot X^{\perp}\right)
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert A_{\lambda}^{\pm d}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\exp\left(\mp\operatorname{i}\frac{1}{2}b\lambda d\cdot X^{\perp}\right)A_{\lambda}\exp\left(\pm\operatorname{i}\frac{1}{2}b\lambda d\cdot X^{\perp}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(Q_{\pm d}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\exp\left(\mp\operatorname{i}\frac{1}{2}b\lambda d\cdot X^{\perp}\right)\left(\chi_{B_{3a}(0)}(X)+\chi_{B_{3a}(0)}(X)^{\perp}\right)A_{\lambda}\right\rVert\exp\left(\frac{b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|}{2}\left\lVert d\right\rVert a\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(3\frac{b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|}{2}\left\lVert d\right\rVert a\right)\left\lVert A_{\lambda}\right\rVert\exp\left(\frac{b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|}{2}\left\lVert d\right\rVert a\right)+

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\exp\left(\mp\operatorname{i}\frac{1}{2}b\lambda d\cdot X^{\perp}\right)\chi_{B_{3a}(0)}(X)^{\perp}A_{\lambda}\right\rVert\exp\left(\frac{b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|}{2}\left\lVert d\right\rVert a\right)\,.

Now using
eq.

2.27
controls the first term. To deal with the second term we rewrite

\displaystyle B_{3a}(0)^{c}=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\Set{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}{3a+n\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert<3a+n+1}

so that, invoking
item

4
, we get

\displaystyle\left\lVert\exp\left(\mp\operatorname{i}\frac{1}{2}b\lambda d\cdot X^{\perp}\right)\chi_{B_{3a}(0)}(X)^{\perp}A_{\lambda}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\exp\left(\frac{b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|}{2}\left\lVert d\right\rVert\left(3a+n+1\right)-c\left|\lambda\right|\left(a+n\right)\right)

so that invoking again the assumption that
b
is sufficiently small, we find

\displaystyle\left\lVert A_{\lambda}^{\pm d}\right\rVert\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)

indeed.

Using
A_{\lambda}^{\pm d}
, we construct an approximate
R_{\lambda}(z_{\lambda})Q_{-d,d}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{A_{\lambda}^{-d,d}}:=A_{\lambda}^{d}Q_{d}+A_{\lambda}^{-d}Q_{-d}\,.

Then

\displaystyle\left(H_{\lambda}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\widetilde{A_{\lambda}^{-d,d}}=Q_{-d,d}+\lambda^{2}v(X+d)A_{\lambda}^{d}Q_{d}+\lambda^{2}v(X-d)A_{\lambda}^{-d}Q_{-d}\,.

The correction terms

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}v(X+d)A_{\lambda}^{d}Q_{d}+\lambda^{2}v(X-d)A_{\lambda}^{-d}Q_{-d}

can be made arbitrarily small thanks to
item

4
as
v(X+d)
is supported within
Q_{-d}
. Hence the desired operator

\displaystyle A_{\lambda}^{-d,d}:=\widetilde{A_{\lambda}^{-d,d}}\left(Q_{-d,d}+\lambda^{2}v(X+d)A_{\lambda}^{d}Q_{d}+\lambda^{2}v(X-d)A_{\lambda}^{-d}Q_{-d}\right)^{-1}

exists and is analytic.

The bound on its derivatives is obtained from the
\alpha>0
case of
item

4
.
∎

2.3.2
Spanning the two dimensional space
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}

We define our two trial functions

\displaystyle\Phi_{\pm d}:=Q_{\pm d}\widehat{R}^{\pm d}\chi(X)\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}

(2.48)

with
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}
as in
eq.

E.8
and the magnetic translations defined in
eq.

1.5
. Here
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,1])
is some cut-off function which equals
1
on, say
B_{a/2}(0)
and zero on
B_{3a/4}(0)^{c}
so that the projection onto
Q_{\pm d}
is actually redundant. We use
\chi
so that
\Phi_{\pm d}
would remain smooth even with its compact support (with
Q_{\pm d}
alone this would of course not be the case).

With the quasimodes
\Phi_{\pm d}
we now define our basis for
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
as

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm d}:=\Pi_{\lambda}Q_{-d,d}\Phi_{\pm d}

(2.49)

These are two vectors within
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
that are not orthonormal.

Lemma 2.6
.

For
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R}
,

\psi_{\pm d}
are linearly independent and nearly orthonormal. In particular,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi_{\alpha},\psi_{\beta}\rangle-\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\right|\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\qquad(\alpha,\beta=\pm d)\,.

(2.50)

Proof.

For simplicity we ignore
\chi
in the definition of
\Phi_{\pm d}
, since it is obvious how to correct for it. Using
E.2
below we see that for real
\lambda
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Pi_{\lambda}^{\perp}\widehat{R}^{\pm d}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\right\rVert\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\,.

Hence

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Pi_{\lambda}^{\perp}\Phi_{\pm d}\right\rVert\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}+\left\lVert\Pi_{\lambda}^{\perp}Q_{\pm d}^{\perp}\widehat{R}^{\pm d}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\right\rVert\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\,.

We thus learn that

\displaystyle\left|1-\left\lVert\psi_{\pm d}\right\rVert^{2}\right|\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\,.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi_{-d},\psi_{d}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\langle\Phi_{-d},\Pi_{\lambda}\Phi_{d}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\langle Q_{-d}\widehat{R}^{-d}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO},\Pi_{\lambda}Q_{d}\widehat{R}^{d}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\langle Q_{-d}\widehat{R}^{-d}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO},\Pi_{\lambda}^{\perp}Q_{d}\widehat{R}^{d}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\,.

∎

Corollary 2.7
.

The matrix
G
defined above in
eq.

2.38
obeys, for large real
\lambda
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert G-\mathds{1}\right\rVert\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\,.

2.3.3
Obtaining the splitting

Consider the Gramian
2\times 2
matrix
G
defined in
eq.

2.38
. Since
G>0
, we know that
G^{-1/2}
orthonormalizes our basis
\psi_{\pm d}
, so that
G^{-1/2}MG^{-1/2}
yields the restriction of
\Pi_{\lambda}H_{\lambda}\Pi_{\lambda}
in the orthonormal basis associated to
\psi_{\pm d}
, and thus by the usual formula for a
2\times 2
matrix,

\displaystyle\left(E_{1}-E_{0}\right)^{2}=\left(\operatorname{tr}\left(G^{-1/2}MG^{-1/2}\right)\right)^{2}-4\det\left(G^{-1/2}MG^{-1/2}\right)\,.

(2.51)

As we explained this formula is inconvenient for our purposes since it is not manifestly analytic.

Thus, we multiply this equation by
\left(\det G\right)^{2}
and use
\operatorname{adj}\left(G\right)=\det\left(G\right)G^{-1}
to get

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}\left(E_{1}-E_{0}\right)^{2}=\left(\operatorname{tr}\left(\operatorname{adj}(G)M\right)\right)^{2}-4\left(\det G\right)\det M

(2.52)

with
\gamma_{\lambda}:=\det G
.
This holds as long as
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R}
and we take it as the definition of the analytic continuation of the LHS for
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
. From
2.7
we have

\displaystyle\left|\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}-1\right|\lesssim\lambda^{-1/2}\qquad(\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\cap\mathbb{R})\,.

(2.53)

We see that the RHS of
eq.

2.52
is a polynomial in the matrix elements of
G
and
M
, each of which is an analytic function of
\lambda
since
\psi_{\pm d}
manifestly is, so we are left with establishing the bounds on these matrix elements of
G
and
M
for
\lambda\in\Omega_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
.

The appearance of the magnetic translations with complex
\lambda
deteriorates any bound to an exponentially diverging one:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\psi_{d}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\Pi_{\lambda}Q_{-d,d}\right\rVert\left\lVert\widehat{R}^{d}Q\varphi_{\lambda}^{\rm MHO}\right\rVert

and
both
terms have upper bounds of the form
\lambda^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)
.

To bound the matrix elements of
M
, we note that the existence of the cut off
\chi
in the definition of
\Phi_{\pm d}
guarantees the latter is smooth. Moreover, using
eq.

2.44
with
\alpha=0,1,2
we get

\displaystyle\left\lVert P^{\alpha}_{j}\psi_{\pm d}\right\rVert\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(Cb\left|\lambda\right|\right)\qquad(j=1,2;\,\alpha=0,1,2)\,.

(2.54)

Indeed, for
\alpha=1,2
the action of
P
on
\psi_{\pm d}
follows the Leibniz rule. Since
\chi
has support within
B_{a/2}(0)
the projections
Q_{\pm d}
are in fact redundant. When
P_{j}
hits
\Pi_{\lambda}
we use the Riesz representation to estimate
PA_{\lambda}^{-d,d}
and then use
eq.

2.44
.

With
eq.

2.54
, the matrix elements of
M
indeed have the necessary upper bound. The proof of
2.4
is now complete.

3
Lower bounds on analytic functions

In this section we shall derive
lower bounds
on functions which are analytic in a region of the complex plane, in the case where we have upper bounds throughout a region and a lower bound at
at a single point
.
Denote the open unit disc and open right half plane by

\mathbb{D}\equiv\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}:\left|z\right|<1}\quad{\rm and}\quad\mathbb{H}\equiv\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{z\right\}>0}.

Our main result is the following:

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
F:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
(hence
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}}|F(z)|\leq 1
) be an analytic function such that for a given
\beta>0
,

\displaystyle\left|F(1)\right|\geq\operatorname{e}^{-\beta}\,.

(3.1)

Then there exists a constant
C<\infty
(independent of
F
and
\beta
) such that for all
\delta\in(0,1/4)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq C\frac{\beta}{\delta}

(3.2)

and moreover, there exists a constant
\mu_{0}\geq 0
such that as
\delta\to 0^{+}
we also have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}\left|-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)-\frac{1}{t}\mu_{0}\right|\operatorname{d}{t}=o\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\,.

(3.3)

A key tool in the proof of
3.1
is the Blashcke factorization theorem
[
20
, Theorem 15.21]
or
[
13
, Section G.3]
. While the theorem is typically stated for functions defined on the unit disc, for our purposes it is convenient to state it in the half-plane.

Theorem 3.2

(Blaschke factorization theorem)
.

Let

\displaystyle F:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}

be analytic such that
