F(1)\neq 0
. Further, let
\Set{a_{\nu}}_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
denote its zeros, listed with multiplicity. Then the following hold:

1.

The sum

\quad\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}-\log\left(\left|\frac{1-a_{\nu}}{1+\overline{a_{\nu}}}\right|\right)\quad

converges.

2.

Let phases
\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta_{\nu}}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
be chosen so that

\displaystyle\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta_{\nu}}\frac{1-a_{\nu}}{1+\overline{a_{\nu}}}>0\,.

Then the product
\prod_{\nu\geq 1}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta_{\nu}}\frac{z-a_{\nu}}{z+\overline{a_{\nu}}}

converges uniformly as
z
varies in compact subsets of
\mathbb{H}
. Hence we have an analytic function
B:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
given by

\displaystyle B(z):=\prod_{\nu\geq 1}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta_{\nu}}\frac{z-a_{\nu}}{z+\overline{a_{\nu}}}\qquad(z\in\mathbb{H})\,.

(3.4)

3.

There exists an analytic function
G:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
such that

F
has the factorization

\displaystyle F=B\operatorname{e}^{-G}\,.

(3.5)

Moreover,
\mathbb{H}\ni z\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G(z)\right\}
is non-negative and harmonic.

Remark 3.3
.

We remark that: (a)
B
vanishes precisely at the points
\{a_{\nu}\}_{\nu\geq 1}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
, with the same multiplicities as the zeros of
F
,
(b)
B
satisfies the bound
|B(z)|<1
for
z\in\mathbb{H}
(unless
F
is a constant function of absolute value
1
), and (c)

|B(z)|\to 1
as
z\in\mathbb{H}
as
z\in\operatorname{i}\mathbb{R}
a.e. and non-tangentially.

For
3.1
we seek lower bounds on
|F|
. By
3.2
, this question is equivalent to upper bounds for the positive expressions:

-\log|F|=-\log|B|+\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G\right\}.

We divide our task into a derivation of, separately, upper bounds for

-\log|B|
, and for the non-negative harmonic function
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G\right\}
.

Further, in
3.1
we assume a lower bound at one point,
|F(1)|\geq\operatorname{e}^{-\beta}
. Hence, using the properties of
B
and
G
in
3.2
, we have the following
a priori
information:

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}-\log\left(\left|\frac{1-a_{\nu}}{1+\overline{a_{\nu}}}\right|\right)\leq\beta\quad{\rm and}\quad\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G(1)\right\}\leq\beta\,.

(3.6)

We now embark on the proof of
3.1
. The bounds
eq.

3.6
play a central role.

3.1
Estimates on
B

In this section we shall prove the following

Theorem 3.4

(Blaschke product estimates)
.

Let
\Set{a_{\nu}}_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
be a sequence of points such that for some given
\beta\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}:\left|a_{\nu}\right|<1}\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a_{\nu}\right\}&<\beta\\
\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}:\left|a_{\nu}\right|\geq 1}\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{\nu}}\right\}&<\beta\,.\end{split}

(3.7)

Then the (convergent by (
3.7
)) Blaschke product

\displaystyle B(z):=\prod_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\theta_{\nu}}\frac{z-a_{\nu}}{z+\overline{a_{\nu}}}\qquad(z\in\mathbb{H}).

(3.8)

obeys the estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|B(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\beta}{\delta},\quad(\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{4}))

(3.9)

for some universal constant
C
(independent of
\Set{a_{\nu}}_{\nu},\beta
and
\delta
). Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|B(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}=o\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\,\textrm{as $\delta\to 0^{+}$ }.

(3.10)

Remark 3.5
.

We shall see below that the summability conditions
eq.

3.7
are a consequence of the first bound in
eq.

3.6
.

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to the proof of
3.4
.
The proof will be divided into two parts, based on a decomposition into Blaschke subproducts associated with the zeros of
B
which are "small" in magnitude and those which are "large". We let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}:=\Set{\nu\in\mathbb{N}:\left|a_{\nu}\right|<1}\,,\qquad\mathcal{B}:=\mathbb{N}\setminus\mathcal{S}=\Set{\nu\in\mathbb{N}:\left|a_{\nu}\right|\geq 1}\,.

(3.11)

For convenience we denote the single Blaschke factor (without phases), which vanishes at
z=a
, by

\displaystyle B_{a}(z):=\frac{z-a}{z+\overline{a}}\qquad(a,z\in\mathbb{H}),

(3.12)

and set

\displaystyle B_{\mathcal{S}}:=\prod_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\operatorname{e}^{i\theta_{\nu}}B_{a_{\nu}},\quad B_{\mathcal{B}}:=\prod_{\nu\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{e}^{i\theta_{\nu}}B_{a_{\nu}}.

(3.13)

Then, the Blaschke product may be written as

B=B_{\mathcal{S}}\ B_{\mathcal{B}}.

Note that for fixed
a\in\mathbb{H}
,
B_{a}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
is a conformal mapping which vanishes only at
z=a
and which maps
\partial\mathbb{H}
to
\partial\mathbb{D}
. Hence
\mathbb{H}\ni z\mapsto-\log\left(\left|B(z)\right|\right)
is non-negative and a finite
upper bound
on it implies a strictly positive exponential lower bound on
|B(z)|
.

To prove
3.4
we establish
eq.

3.9
and
eq.

3.10
separately for
B_{\mathcal{S}}
and
B_{\mathcal{B}}
.

3.1.1
The "small" Blaschke product,
B_{\mathcal{S}}

To study the product
B_{\mathcal{S}}
it will be convenient to have some elementary estimates on the single factors.

Lemma 3.6

(Estimate on a single Blaschke factor)
.

Let
t>0
and
a\in\mathbb{H}
. Then

1.

We have

\displaystyle-\log\left(\left|B_{a}(t)\right|\right)\leq-\log\left(\left|B_{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a\right\}}(t)\right|\right)\,.

(3.14)

2.

Assume that

\textrm{either}\qquad 0<\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a\right\}}{t}<\frac{1}{2}\quad{\rm or}\quad 2<\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a\right\}}{t}<\infty.

Then,

\displaystyle-\log\left(\left|B_{a}(t)\right|\right)\leq 4\min\left(\Set{\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a\right\}}{t},\frac{t}{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a\right\}}}\right)\,.

(3.15)

Proof.

It is convenient to introduce, for
x>0
, the shorthand:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\left(x\right):=\min\Set{x,\frac{1}{x}}=x\ \chi_{(0,1)}(x)+\frac{1}{x}\ \chi_{[1,\infty)}(x)\ .

(3.16)

Let us write
a=:\alpha+\operatorname{i}\beta
with
\alpha>0
, since
a\in\mathbb{H}
. Then

\displaystyle 1-\left|B_{a}(t)\right|^{2}=\frac{4\alpha t}{(t+\alpha)^{2}+\beta^{2}}\leq\frac{4\alpha t}{(t+\alpha)^{2}}=1-\left|B_{\alpha}(t)\right|^{2}

(3.17)

from which
eq.

3.14
follows. To prove
eq.

3.15
, we first observe a scaling property of
B_{x}(t)
:

\displaystyle\textrm{For $x>0$},\quad\left|B_{x}(t)\right|=\left|\frac{t-x}{t+x}\right|=\left|\frac{1-x/t}{1+x/t}\right|=\left|B_{\frac{x}{t}}(1)\right|.

(3.18)

Therefore, to prove the bound
eq.

3.15
it suffices to bound

-\log\left(B_{x}\right)
for
x=\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a\right\}/t
.

First let
x\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Then,
\left|B_{x}(1)\right|=\frac{1-x}{1+x}
. Since over this range of
x
,
\log(1+x)\leq x
and
-\log(1-x)=\int_{0}^{x}(1-s)^{-1}\operatorname{d}{s}\leq x(1-x)^{-1}=-1+(1-x)^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle-\log\left(\left|B_{x}(1)\right|\right)=\log\left(1+x\right)-\log\left(1-x\right)\leq x-1+\frac{1}{1-x}=\frac{x(2-x)}{1-x}\leq 2x(2-x)\leq 4x\,.

The case
x>2
follows at once from the case
x<1/2
since
B_{x}(1)=B_{1/x}(1)
.

∎

We now turn to the lower bound on
B_{\mathcal{S}}
. Recall the notation
\alpha_{\nu}:=\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a_{\nu}\right\}
. By
eq.

3.14
,
-\log\left(\left|B_{\mathcal{S}}(t)\right|\right)\leq\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}-\log\left(\left|B_{\alpha_{\nu}}(t)\right|\right)
. Next, we further decompose this upper bound as follows. For any
t>0
,

\displaystyle-\log\left(\left|B_{\mathcal{S}}(t)\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathcal{S}\\
t/\alpha_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus[1/2,2]\end{subarray}}-\log\left(\left|B_{\alpha_{\nu}}(t)\right|\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathcal{S}\\
t/\alpha_{\nu}\in[1/2,2]\end{subarray}}-\log\left(\left|B_{\alpha_{\nu}}(t)\right|\right)

\displaystyle=:\quad{\rm SUM}_{\mathcal{S},\mathrm{out}}(t)+{\rm SUM}_{\mathcal{S},\mathrm{in}}(t)

(3.19)

Note that by analyticity of
z\mapsto B_{\mathcal{S}}(z)
on
\mathbb{H}
,
{\rm SUM}_{\mathcal{S},\mathrm{in}}(t)
is a finite sum, whereas
{\rm SUM}_{\mathcal{S},\mathrm{out}}(t)
may be an infinite sum.

We’ll now average the bound
eq.

3.19
over some interval
t\in[\delta,2\delta]
of length
\delta>0
. For the first term on the RHS of
eq.

3.19
, we apply
eq.

3.15
and average over an interval
[\delta,2\delta]
to obtain

\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}{\rm SUM}_{\mathcal{S},\mathrm{out}}(t)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq 4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathcal{S}\\
t/\alpha_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus[1/2,2]\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}\mathfrak{m}\left(t/\alpha_{\nu}\right)\operatorname{d}{t}.

We shall apply the following

Lemma 3.7
.

For any
\alpha>0
,
\delta>0
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}\mathfrak{m}\left(t/\alpha\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha\right)\,.

(3.20)

Proof.

First we note that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}\left(t/\alpha\right)\equiv\min\Big\{\frac{t}{\alpha},\frac{\alpha}{t}\Big\}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\ t\ \chi_{(0,\alpha]}(t)+\alpha\ \frac{1}{t}\ \chi_{(\alpha,\infty)}(t)\ .

(3.21)

For a fixed
\delta>0
, consider the three cases: (i)
\alpha<\delta
, (ii)
\alpha>2\delta
and (iii)
\delta\leq\alpha\leq 2\delta
.

For the first case, when
\alpha<\delta\leq t\leq 2\delta
,

\mathfrak{m}\left(t/\alpha\right)=\alpha/t
and we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}\mathfrak{m}\left(t/\alpha\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\delta}\left[\log(2\delta)-\log(\delta)\right]=\log(2)\frac{\alpha}{\delta}{=}\log 2\ \mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha\right).

For the second case, when
\delta\leq t\leq 2\delta<\alpha
,
\mathfrak{m}\left(t/\alpha\right)=t/\alpha
and we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}\mathfrak{m}\left(t/\alpha\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta\alpha}\frac{1}{2}\left((2\delta)^{2}-\delta^{2}\right)=\frac{3}{2}\frac{\delta}{\alpha}=\frac{3}{2}\ \mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha\right)\,.

Finally, let
\delta\leq\alpha\leq 2\delta
. Then, we get:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}\mathfrak{m}\left(t/\alpha\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{\alpha}\frac{t}{\alpha}\operatorname{d}{t}+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\alpha}^{2\delta}\frac{\alpha}{t}\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\delta\alpha}(\alpha^{2}-\delta^{2})+\frac{\alpha}{\delta}\log\left(\frac{2\delta}{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\alpha}{\delta}+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}\right)

(
\log(x)\leq x-1
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\frac{\delta}{\alpha}=\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha\right)\,.

(
0\leq\left(\alpha/\delta-2\right)^{2}
)

The bounds over regions (i), (ii) and (iii) imply
eq.

3.20
.
∎

Applying
3.7
we have

\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}{\rm SUM}_{\mathcal{S},\mathrm{out}}(t)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq 6\ \sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha_{\nu}\right).

(3.22)

Next, averaging the second term in
eq.

3.19
, we prefer to convert the characteristic function of
t/\alpha_{\nu}\in[1/2,2]
into one which does not depend on
t
but rather only on
\delta
. The characteristic function is equivalent to the requirement that
\alpha\in t[1/2,2]
. But
t\in[\delta,2\delta]
already, so
t[1/2,2]\subseteq\delta[1/2,4]
. Once we get rid of the
t
dependence in the characteristic function we are left with evaluating the integral. We find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}{\rm SUM}_{\mathcal{S},\mathrm{in}}(t)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}\ \sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\chi_{[1/2,2]}(t/\alpha_{\nu})\ \left(-\log\Big|\frac{t-\alpha_{\nu}}{t+\alpha_{\nu}}\Big|\ \right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\chi_{[1/4,4](\frac{\alpha_{\nu}}{\delta})}\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}\ \left(-\log\Big|\frac{t-\alpha_{\nu}}{t+\alpha_{\nu}}\Big|\ \right)\operatorname{d}{t}\,.

Note that

\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}\ \left(-\log\Big|\frac{t-\alpha_{\nu}}{t+\alpha_{\nu}}\Big|\ \right)\operatorname{d}{t}=\int_{1}^{2}\ \left(-\log\Big|\frac{s-\frac{\alpha_{\nu}}{\delta}}{s+\frac{\alpha_{\nu}}{\delta}}\Big|\ \right)\operatorname{d}{s}\leq C_{\mathrm{in}},

where

C_{\mathrm{in}}:=\sup_{y\in[1/4,4]}\int_{1}^{2}\ \left(-\log\Big|\frac{s-y}{s+y}\Big|\ \right)\operatorname{d}{s}\leq 3\,.

This last estimate is obtained, for instance by numerical integration.

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}{\rm SUM}_{\mathcal{S},\mathrm{in}}(t)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq 3\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}\,:\,\alpha_{\nu}\in[\delta/4,4\delta]}\ 1

(3.23)

which is finite by analyticity of
B_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Combining
eq.

3.23
together with
eq.

3.22
we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|B_{\mathcal{S}}(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq 6\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha_{\nu}\right)+3\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S},\ \alpha_{\nu}\in[\delta/4,4\delta]}\ 1.

We next re-express the last sum in terms of
\mathfrak{m}
. Note that if
\delta/4\leq\alpha\leq 4\delta
, then

1/4\leq\mathfrak{m}\left(\alpha/\delta\right)\leq 4
and hence
1\leq 4\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha\right)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|B_{\mathcal{S}}(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq\left(6+12\right)\ \sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha_{\nu}\right).

(3.24)

To prove the bound
eq.

3.9
for the “small Blaschke product",
B_{\mathcal{S}}
,
we use
\mathfrak{m}\left(x\right)\leq x^{-1}
, to obtain from
eq.

3.24

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|B_{\mathcal{S}}(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq 18\delta^{-1}\ \sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{\nu}\leq 18\frac{\beta}{\delta},

(3.25)

where in the last step we used the summability hypothesis
eq.

3.7
.

Let us now do a more refined analysis of the upper bound
eq.

3.24
to prove
eq.

3.10
for
B_{\mathcal{S}}
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, we will show that there exists a
\delta
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha_{\nu}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}

and hence the result. Fix some
\xi<1
. We then divide
\mathcal{S}
further into two sets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\xi}\sqcup\left(\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\xi}\right)\,,\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\xi}:=\Set{\nu\in\mathbb{N}:\alpha_{\nu}<\delta^{\xi}}\,.

On
\mathcal{S}_{\xi}
: The sequence
\Set{\chi_{(0,\delta^{\xi})}(\alpha_{\nu})\alpha_{\nu}}_{\delta>0}
converges to zero as
\delta\to 0^{+}
for fixed
\nu
. Moreover, it is summable at
\delta=\infty
thanks to
eq.

3.7
. If a series converges, then as
n\to\infty
, the sum of all terms past term
n
tends to zero. Hence,

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\chi_{(0,\delta^{\xi})}(\alpha_{\nu})\alpha_{\nu}=0\,.

On the set
\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\xi}
, we have
\alpha_{\nu}\geq\delta^{\xi}>\delta
so
\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha_{\nu}\right)=\frac{\delta}{\alpha_{\nu}}\leq\frac{\alpha_{\nu}}{\delta^{2\xi-1}}
and so

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\xi}}\mathfrak{m}\left(\alpha_{\nu}/\delta\right)\leq\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{\alpha_{\nu}}{\delta^{2\xi-1}}\leq\frac{\beta}{\delta^{2\xi-1}}\,.

Combining these two together we find that for any
\varepsilon>0
there is some
\delta_{\varepsilon}>0
such that if
\delta<\delta_{\varepsilon}
then

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha_{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{\xi}}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha_{\nu}\right)+\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\xi}}\mathfrak{m}\left(\delta/\alpha_{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{S}_{\xi}}\alpha_{\nu}+\frac{\beta}{\delta^{2\xi-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}+\frac{\beta}{\delta^{2\xi-1}}=\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\varepsilon+\beta\delta^{2\left(1-\xi\right)}\right)\,.

Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary and
\xi<1
we obtain the result.

3.1.2
The "big" Blaschke product,
B_{\mathcal{B}}

Finally we tend to bounding
B_{\mathcal{B}}
, where
\mathcal{B}\equiv\Set{\nu\in\mathbb{N}:\left|a_{\nu}\right|\geq 1}
. Note that due to the scaling property
eq.

3.18
we have

\displaystyle-\log\left(\left|B_{\mathcal{B}}(t)\right|\right)=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{B}}-\log\left(\left|B_{a_{\nu}}(t)\right|\right)=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{B}}-\log\left(\left|B_{a_{\nu}^{-1}}(t^{-1})\right|\right).

Since
z\mapsto z^{-1}
maps
\mathbb{R}^{+}
to
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
\mathbb{H}
to
\mathbb{H}
, we apply part 2 of
3.6
and deduce that

\textrm{if
$0<t\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{\nu}}\right\}<1/2$,\quad then }\quad-\log\left(\left|B_{a_{\nu}^{-1}}(t^{-1})\right|\right)\leq 4\mathfrak{m}\left(t\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{\nu}}\right\}\right)=4t\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{\nu}}\right\}.

The above condition holds for
0<t<1/2
and
|a_{\nu}|\geq 1
since

0<t\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{\nu}}\right\}=t\frac{\alpha_{\nu}}{|a_{\nu}|^{2}}\leq t<1/2.

Therefore, using the assumed summability of
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a_{\nu}^{-1}\right\}
in
eq.

3.7
, we have

\displaystyle-\log\left(\left|B_{\mathcal{B}}(t)\right|\right)\leq 4t\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{\nu}}\right\}<4t\beta\ .

Finally, we average the previous bound over the interval
[\delta,2\delta]
and obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|B_{\mathcal{B}}(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq 6\beta\delta\,.

The proof of
3.4
is now complete. ∎

3.2
Bounds on
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G\right\}

Recall from
3.2
that
F=e^{-G}\ B
, where
B
is a Blaschke product and
G:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
analytic with
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G\right\}\geq 0
. And therefore, to bound on
|F|
from below on
\mathbb{H}
, we need upper bounds on
-\log|B|
and on
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G\right\}
on
\mathbb{H}
. Upper bounds (on average) for
-\log|B|
were proved in the previous section. We now turn to upper bounds on
G_{R}:=\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G\right\}
.

Since
G_{R}:\mathbb{H}\to[0,\infty)
is harmonic it has a representation as a Poisson integral with respect to a finite non-negative measure
\mu
and some constant
A\geq 0
(see e.g.
[
24
, Chapter 3 on the Herglotz represenation]
):
For any

x+\operatorname{i}y\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle G_{R}(x+\operatorname{i}y)=Ax+\int_{\psi\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{\left(y-\psi\right)^{2}+x^{2}}\operatorname{d}{\mu(\psi)}.

(3.26)

We note that

\displaystyle 0<G_{R}(1)=A+\int_{\psi\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\psi^{2}+1}\operatorname{d}{\mu(\psi)}\leq\beta,

(3.27)

by the second bound in
eq.

3.6
.

We shall make use of the following

Theorem 3.8
.

Suppose that
\mu
is a positive measure on
\mathbb{R}
such that there exists
\beta\in(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\psi\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\psi^{2}+1}\operatorname{d}{\mu(\psi)}<\beta\,.

(3.28)

Define
u:\mathbb{H}\to[0,\infty)
via

\displaystyle u(t+\operatorname{i}y):=\int_{\psi\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{t}{\left(y-\psi\right)^{2}+t^{2}}\operatorname{d}{\mu(\psi)}\qquad(t+\operatorname{i}y\in\mathbb{H})\,.

(3.29)

Then, for

0<t<1
:

\displaystyle 0\leq u(t)\leq\frac{\beta}{t}\,.

(3.30)

Furthermore, for any
\varepsilon>0
, if
t>0
is sufficiently small, then

\displaystyle u(t)-\frac{1}{t}\mu(\Set{0})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{t}\,.

(3.31)

Proof.

Using the elementary bound

\displaystyle\frac{t}{t^{2}+\psi^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{t}\frac{1}{1+\psi^{2}},\qquad\textrm{for $0<t<1$,}

(3.32)

we obtain
eq.

3.30
as follows: For
0<t<1
,

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\psi\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{t}{t^{2}+\psi^{2}}\operatorname{d}{\mu(\psi)}\leq\frac{1}{t}\int_{\psi\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+\psi^{2}}\operatorname{d}{\mu(\psi)}\leq\frac{\beta}{t}\,.

We next turn the proof of
eq.

3.31
. Let
\eta>0
be given. Then we decompose

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}=\Set{0}\sqcup\left([-\eta,\eta]\setminus\Set{0}\right)\sqcup\left(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-\eta,\eta]\right)\,.

and, correspondingly, in the Poisson integral
eq.

3.29
defining
u
.
We obtain, for
0<t<1
:

\displaystyle u(t)\leq\frac{1}{t}\mu(\Set{0})+\frac{1}{t}\int_{0<|\psi|\leq\eta}\frac{1}{1+\psi^{2}}\operatorname{d}{\mu(\psi)}+\frac{t(1+\eta^{2})}{t^{2}+\eta^{2}}\beta\ .

For the second term we used
eq.

3.32
and for the third term we used

\displaystyle\frac{t(1+\psi^{2})}{t^{2}+\psi^{2}}\leq\frac{t(1+\eta^{2})}{t^{2}+\eta^{2}}\Longleftrightarrow\left(1-t^{2}\right)\left(\eta^{2}-\psi^{2}\right)\leq 0,\quad\textrm{for $\left|\psi\right|\geq\eta$ and $0<t<1$}\,.

Next, given any
\varepsilon>0
, we can pick
\eta_{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small so that

\displaystyle\int_{0<|\psi|\leq\eta_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{1+\psi^{2}}\operatorname{d}{\mu(\psi)}\leq\varepsilon/2\,.

Fixing
\eta_{\varepsilon}
, we now choose
t>0
sufficiently small, so that
\frac{t(1+\eta_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{t^{2}+\eta_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\beta\leq\varepsilon/2
.
Hence, for all
t>0
sufficiently small,
0\leq u(t)-t^{-1}\mu(\Set{0})\ \leq\ \varepsilon t^{-1}
. The proof of
3.8
is now complete.
∎

3.3
Proof of
3.1
; lower bound on
|F|
for
F:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
analytic.

In this section we combine the results of the two preceding sections,
3.4
and
3.8
to give a

Proof of
3.1
.

Recall that

1.

By
3.2
,
F
factors into a Blaschke product,
B
, and a non-vanishing analytic function,
\operatorname{e}^{-G}
, both analytic on
\mathbb{H}
:

\displaystyle F(z)=\operatorname{e}^{-G(z)}\prod_{\nu\geq 1}B_{a_{\nu}}(z)=\operatorname{e}^{-G(z)}B(z),

where
\Set{a_{\nu}}_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}
are the zeros of
F
in
\mathbb{H}
(with multiplicity listed).

2.

The assumed lower bound
eq.

3.1
,
|F(1)|\geq\operatorname{e}^{-\beta}
, implies
the upper bounds
eq.

3.6
:

\displaystyle-\log\left(\left|B_{a}(1)\right|\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}-\log\left(\left|B_{a_{\nu}}(1)\right|\right)<\beta,\ \textrm{and}

(3.33)

\displaystyle 0<\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G(1)\right\}

\displaystyle=G_{R}(1)<\beta

(3.34)

We first show that
eq.

3.33
implies the summability
eq.

3.7
. Write
a_{\nu}=:\alpha_{\nu}+\operatorname{i}\beta_{\nu}
. Using
eq.

3.14
and the bound
-\log y^{2}\geq 1-y^{2}
and
eq.

3.17
for
t=1
, i.e. that
1-|B_{a}(1)|^{2}=4\alpha/[(1+\alpha)^{2}+\beta^{2}]
,

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}-\log\left(\left|B_{a_{\nu}}(1)^{2}\right|\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}\left(1-|B_{a_{\nu}}(1)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|a_{\nu}|\leq 1}\left(1-|B_{a_{\nu}}(1)|^{2}\right)=\sum_{|a_{\nu}|\leq 1}\frac{2\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a_{\nu}\right\}}{(1+\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a_{\nu}\right\})^{2}+\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{a_{\nu}\right\}^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\ \sum_{|a_{\nu}|\leq 1}\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a_{\nu}\right\}\ .

Here, we used that

\displaystyle\frac{2\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{z\right\}}{(1+\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{z\right\})^{2}+\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{z\right\}^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\ \operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{z\right\},\quad\textrm{if $|z|^{2}\leq 1$ and $\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{z\right\}>0$.}

(3.36)

This proves the first summability bound in

eq.

3.7
.

Next, using the first line of
section

3.3
and the scaling property
|B_{a}(1)|=|B_{1/a}(1)|
,
we have

\displaystyle\beta>\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}-\log\left(\left|B_{a_{\nu}}(1)^{2}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|a_{\nu}|\geq 1}\left(1-|B_{a_{\nu}}(1)|^{2}\right)\ =\ \frac{1}{2}\sum_{|1/a_{\nu}|\leq 1}\left(1-|B_{1/a_{\nu}}(1)|^{2}\right)\ .

(3.37)

We may now apply the bound
section

3.3
, with
a_{\nu}
replaced by
1/a_{\nu}
, to
eq.

3.37
and we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\ \sum_{|a_{\nu}|\geq 1}\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{\nu}}\right\}\leq\beta.

This proves the second summability bound in

eq.

3.7
.

Thus, we may apply the bounds:
eq.

3.9
,
eq.

3.30
, and
eq.

3.10
,
eq.

3.31
.
Recall
-\log|F|=G_{R}-\log|B|
. Using
eq.

3.9
and
eq.

3.30
we have, for
\delta\in(0,1/4)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}G_{R}(t)\operatorname{d}{t}+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|B(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}\left(\beta t+\frac{\beta}{t}\right)\operatorname{d}{t}+18\frac{\beta}{\delta}=(18+\log(2)+\frac{3}{2}\delta^{2})\frac{\beta}{\delta},

where the term
\beta t
comes from
Ax
in
eq.

3.26
. This is the first conclusion of
3.1
. The second conclusion of
3.1
is obtained from
eq.

3.10
and
eq.

3.31
as follows:
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}\left|-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)-\frac{1}{t}\mu(\Set{0})\right|\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}\left|-\log\left(\left|B(t)\right|\right)+G_{R}(t)-\frac{1}{t}\mu(\Set{0})\right|\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|B(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}\left(G_{R}(t)-\frac{1}{t}\mu(\Set{0})\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=o\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right),\quad\textrm{as $\delta\to 0^{+}$}.

This completes the proof of
3.1
.∎

3.4
Passing from
\mathbb{H}
to a wedge

Define the wedge in
\Gamma_{\alpha}\subset\overline{\mathbb{H}}
:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha}:=\Set{z\in\mathbb{C}:-\alpha\pi/2<\operatorname{Arg}(z)<\alpha\pi/2},\quad 0<\alpha\leq 1.

Here
\operatorname{Arg}(z)
is the principal value, taking values
-\pi<\operatorname{Arg}(z)\leq\pi
. The mapping
\mathbb{H}\ni z\mapsto z^{-\alpha}\in\Gamma_{\alpha}
is a conformal equivalence (to define its inverse we use the principal branch of the logarithm), so given an analytic mapping
\Gamma_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{D}
, we may precompose it with the map
\left(z\mapsto z^{-\alpha}\right):\mathbb{H}\to\Gamma_{\alpha}
to apply our earlier results on analytic maps
\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
. So, two things are happening here: (i) the lower bounds now apply to analytic functions on a wedge and (ii) the lower bounds apply to large magnitudes within the wedge, since
t\to 0^{+}
is mapped to a neighborhood of infinity (which better suits our applications outside this section).

We obtain

Theorem 3.9

(Lower bounds on analytic functions in a wedge
\Gamma_{\alpha}
)
.

Let
F:\Gamma_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{D}
be analytic and suppose further that for some given
\beta\in(0,\infty)
we have

\displaystyle\left|F(1)\right|\geq\operatorname{e}^{-\beta}\,.

Then there is some constant
C<\infty
(independent of
\alpha,F
and
\beta
) such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{s=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq C\alpha\beta 2^{1/\alpha}R^{1/\alpha}\qquad(R>2^{\alpha})\,.

(3.38)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{s=R}^{2R}\left|-\log\left(\left|F(s)\right|\right)-s^{1/\alpha}\mu_{0}\right|\operatorname{d}{s}\overset{R\to\infty}{=}o\left(R^{1/\alpha}\right)

(3.39)

for some
\mu_{0}\geq 0
.

Proof.

Given
F:\Gamma_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{D}
analytic, introduce its composition with
\mathbb{H}\ni z\mapsto z^{-\alpha}\in\Gamma_{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}(z):=F(z^{-\alpha})\qquad(z\in\mathbb{H})\,,

yielding an analytic function
\widetilde{F}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
satisfying
\left|\widetilde{F}(1)\right|=\left|F(1)\right|\geq\operatorname{e}^{-\beta}
. We may apply
3.1
to
\widetilde{F}
to obtain an upper bound for the average of
-\log\left(\left|\widetilde{F}\right|\right)
over small
t-
intervals in
\mathbb{H}
:

\displaystyle 20\frac{\beta}{\delta}\geq\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{t=\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|\widetilde{F}(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t},\quad\textrm{for $0<\delta<1/4$.}

We then convert the average of
\widetilde{F}
to one over appropriate large real intervals in
\Gamma_{\alpha}
via the change of variables
s:=t^{-\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|\widetilde{F}(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}-\log\left(\left|F(t^{-\alpha})\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}\int_{(2\delta)^{-\alpha}}^{\delta^{-\alpha}}-\log\left(\left|F(s)\right|\right)s^{-1-1/\alpha}\operatorname{d}{s}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha}\delta^{\alpha}\int_{(2\delta)^{-\alpha}}^{\delta^{-\alpha}}-\log\left(\left|F(s)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{s}\,.

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\alpha}(2\delta)^{\alpha}\int_{(2\delta)^{-\alpha}}^{\delta^{-\alpha}}-\log\left(\left|F(s)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{s}\leq 20\frac{\beta}{\delta}\,,\quad\textrm{for $0<\delta<1/4$}.

Set
R:=(2\delta)^{-\alpha}
(
R^{-1}=(2\delta)^{\alpha}
) and correspondingly we take
R>2^{\alpha}
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2^{\alpha}-1)R}\int_{R}^{2^{\alpha}R}-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq 20\frac{\alpha\ \beta}{2^{\alpha}-1}R^{1/\alpha}\,,\quad R>2^{\alpha}.

Since
\alpha\leq 1
, we may cover
[R,2R]
by
m:=\lceil\frac{1}{\alpha}\rceil
segments, i.e.,
[R,2R]=\bigcup_{j=0}^{m-1}[2^{\alpha j}R,2^{\alpha\left(j+1\right)}R]
and so we immediately get the bound, valid for all
R>2^{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{s=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\leq 80\alpha\beta 2^{1/\alpha}R^{1/\alpha}\,.

This completes the proof of
eq.

3.38
.

Finally, let
\widetilde{\mu}
denote the positive measure associated with
z\mapsto\widetilde{G}_{R}(z)\equiv\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{G(z^{-\alpha})\right\}
, which is non-negative and harmonic on
\mathbb{H}
. Then, via the above change of variables,
s=t^{-\alpha}
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\delta}^{2\delta}\left|-\log\left(\left|\widetilde{F}(t)\right|\right)-\frac{1}{t}\widetilde{\mu}\left(\Set{0}\right)\right|\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha\delta}\int_{(2\delta)^{-\alpha}}^{\delta^{-\alpha}}\left|-\log\left(\left|F(s)\right|\right)-s^{1/\alpha}\widetilde{\mu}\left(\Set{0}\right)\right|s^{-1-1/\alpha}\operatorname{d}{s}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{\alpha}(2\delta)^{\alpha}\int_{(2\delta)^{-\alpha}}^{\delta^{-\alpha}}\left|-\log\left(\left|F(s)\right|\right)-s^{1/\alpha}\widetilde{\mu}\left(\Set{0}\right)\right|\operatorname{d}{s}

and the proof of
eq.

3.39
is completed using the above relation between
\delta
and
R
.

The proof of
3.9
is now complete.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

The statement above may be generalized as follows. Let
F:\Gamma_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{C}
be analytic such that for some given
\beta\in(0,\infty)
we have

\displaystyle\left|F(1)\right|\geq\operatorname{e}^{-\beta}

and such that for some analytic
nowhere-zero
function
U:\Gamma_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{C}
we have

\displaystyle\left|F(z)\right|\leq\left|U(z)\right|\qquad(z\in\Gamma_{\alpha})\,.

Then there is constant
C<\infty
(independent of
\alpha,F
and
\beta
) such that for all
R>2^{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha 2^{1/\alpha}\left(\beta+\log\left(\left|U(1)\right|\right)\right)R^{1/\alpha}+

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|U(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\,.

as well as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}\left|-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)+\log\left(\left|U(t)\right|\right)-t^{1/\alpha}\mu_{0}\right|\operatorname{d}{t}\overset{R\to\infty}{=}o\left(R^{1/\alpha}\right)

for some
\mu_{0}\geq 0
.

Outside of this appendix we mainly use this corollary with the function

\displaystyle U(z)=\exp\left(Az\right)\qquad(z\in\Gamma_{\alpha})

where
A>0
is some constant.

Proof.

We apply
3.9
on the function
\widetilde{F}(z):=F(z)/U(z)
which clearly obeys
\left|\widetilde{F}(z)\right|\leq 1
for all
z\in\Gamma_{\alpha}
. The lower bound becomes

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{F}(1)\right|\geq\operatorname{e}^{-\left(\beta+\log\left(\left|U(1)\right|\right)\right)}\,.

Then

\displaystyle 80\alpha 2^{1/\alpha}\left(\beta+\log\left(\left|U(1)\right|\right)\right)R^{1/\alpha}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|\widetilde{F}(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|F(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}-\frac{1}{R}\int_{t=R}^{2R}-\log\left(\left|U(t)\right|\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\,.

∎

4
Mesoscopic annuli

Our goal here is to construct the operators
T_{\lambda,\nu}
as in
section

2
, establish bounds on their norms as well as on how well they approximate inverses of
h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}
.

We work on the annuli

\displaystyle U_{\nu}:=\Set{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}{2^{\nu-3}\delta<\left\lVert x\right\rVert<2^{\nu+3}\delta}

where
\nu=0,\cdots,\lceil\log_{2}(R/\delta)\rceil
and
R
is some order
1
parameter. Recall that these open sets are chosen so as to contain

\displaystyle\Set{x}{2^{\nu-2}\delta\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 2^{\nu+2}\delta}\supseteq\operatorname{supp}(\psi_{\nu})\,.

The symbol of
h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}
in
U_{\nu}
is given by

\displaystyle U_{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,p)\mapsto s_{\lambda}^{\theta}(x,p)

\displaystyle:=\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{4}b^{2}\lambda^{2}\theta(x)^{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}-b\lambda\theta(x)p\cdot x^{\perp}+\frac{\operatorname{i}}{2}b\lambda\left(\nabla\theta\right)(x)\cdot x^{\perp}+

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda^{2}\left(v(x)+1\right)-\mu\lambda

(4.1)

for some order
1
complex number
\mu
. Hence
\operatorname{Op}\left(s^{\theta}_{\lambda}\right)=h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}
when restricted to
L^{2}(U_{\nu})
.

Before we proceed, we must recast the problem. Indeed, since our goal is to apply
A.5
, both the domain of the our symbol as well as the symbol itself depend on an asymptotic parameter
\left|\lambda\right|
. As such, it is convenient to
rescale
space so as to apply
A.5
on a symbol is defined on a domain of diameter of order
1
, so as to make the asymptotic dependence on the estimates explicit. To that end, with
\alpha:=2^{\nu}\delta
,
U:=\frac{1}{\alpha}U_{\nu}
is the
fixed
annulus
\Set{\frac{1}{8}<\left\lVert x\right\rVert<8}
. Using
eq.

A.13
, consider the symbol

\displaystyle U\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,p)\mapsto\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}(x,p)

\displaystyle:=\alpha^{2}s_{\lambda}^{\theta}(\alpha x,\frac{1}{\alpha}p)

\displaystyle=\left(p-\frac{1}{2}b\alpha^{2}\lambda\theta(\alpha x)x^{\perp}\right)^{2}+\frac{\operatorname{i}}{2}b\alpha^{3}\lambda\left(\nabla\theta\right)(\alpha x)\cdot x^{\perp}

\displaystyle\qquad+\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}\left(\frac{v(\alpha x)+1}{\alpha^{2}}\right)-\mu\alpha^{2}\lambda

We begin with a basic estimate about the size of
v
. In principle its size could vary both radially and azimuthally within
U_{\nu}
, but we are only interested in bounding the radial variations which cannot vanish or rise too quickly. Within
U_{\nu}
, since
v
is smooth with a unique non-degenerate minimum at
0
, we know that

\displaystyle c_{v}2^{2\nu}\delta^{2}\leq v(x)+1\leq C_{v}2^{2\nu}\delta^{2}\qquad(x\in U_{\nu})

for
C_{v},c_{v}\in(0,\infty)
dependent on
v
. As a result,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}c_{v}\leq w(x)\leq 16C_{v}\qquad(x\in U)

for

\displaystyle w(x):=\frac{v(\alpha x)+1}{\alpha^{2}}\,.

(4.2)

Let
M_{\nu}:=\alpha^{2}\Lambda
. According to the choice of
\delta
in
eq.

2.19
, this implies
M_{\nu}=2^{2\nu}\Lambda^{2\eta}\gg 1
for all
\nu=1,\cdots,\lceil\log_{2}(R/\delta)\rceil
.

We aim to prove that the symbol
\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}(x,p)
is elliptic in
U
in the sense of
A.4
.
This boils down to two ingredients. The first is an upper bound on the derivatives of the form

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{p}^{\beta}\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}(x,p)\right|\leq{\rm const.}\left(M_{\nu}+\left\lVert p\right\rVert\right)^{2-\left|\beta\right|}\qquad(x\in U,p\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{2})\,.

(4.3)

The proof of
eq.

4.3
is clear.

Next, we require a lower bound of the form

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}(x,p)\right|\geq{\rm const.}\left(M_{\nu}+\left\lVert p\right\rVert\right)^{2}\qquad(x\in U,p\in\mathbb{R}^{2})\,.

(4.4)

Towards
eq.

4.4
, we claim that

\displaystyle\left|\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}w(x)\right|\geq\frac{c_{v}\sin(2\varepsilon)}{1+2C_{v}}\left(\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+M_{\nu}^{2}\right)\,.

(4.5)

Indeed, if
\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}\geq 2C_{v}M_{\nu}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}w(x)\right|\geq\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}-C_{v}M_{\nu}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}\geq\frac{C_{v}}{1+2C_{v}}\left(\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+M_{\nu}^{2}\right)

Next, note that we always have, from the fact that
\left|\arg(\lambda)\right|<\pi/2-\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}w(x)\right|\geq\sin(2\varepsilon)\alpha^{2}\left|\lambda\right|^{2}\left(v(\alpha x)+1\right)\geq\frac{c_{v}}{16}\sin(2\varepsilon)M_{\nu}^{2}\,.

As a result, if we now assume
\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}<2C_{v}M_{\nu}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}w(x)\right|\geq c_{v}\sin(2\varepsilon)M_{\nu}^{2}\geq\frac{c_{v}\sin(2\varepsilon)}{1+2C_{v}}\left(\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+M_{\nu}^{2}\right)

We now return to
eq.

4.4
. We have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}(x,p)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+\alpha^{4}\lambda^{2}w(x)\right|-\frac{1}{4}b^{2}\alpha^{4}\left|\lambda\right|^{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}-b\alpha^{2}\left|\lambda\right|\left\lVert x\right\rVert\left\lVert p\right\rVert-\left|\mu\right|\alpha^{2}\left|\lambda\right|-\frac{1}{2}b\left|\lambda\right|\alpha^{3}\left\lVert\nabla\theta(\alpha x)\right\rVert\left\lVert x\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c_{v}\sin(2\varepsilon)}{1+2C_{v}}\left(\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+M_{\nu}^{2}\right)-16b^{2}M_{\nu}^{2}-4b\left(M_{\nu}^{2}+\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}\right)-\left|\mu\right|M_{\nu}-4bM_{\nu}\alpha\left\lVert\nabla\theta\right\rVert_{\infty}

(4.6)

so that
if
b>0
is sufficiently small
and
M_{\nu}
is sufficiently large, clearly we get
eq.

4.4
.

As a result, we may invoke
A.5
on the symbol

\displaystyle U\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,p)\mapsto\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}(x,p)\,.

We invoke it with the choice
\widetilde{\chi}_{\nu}:=\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}\chi_{\nu}\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}^{\ast}
with
\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}
as in
eq.

A.12
, so it is supported in a proper relatively compact subset
V
of
U
.

In particular it yields some operator, say
\widetilde{T}_{\lambda,\nu}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U))
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\operatorname{Op}\left(\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}\right)\widetilde{T}_{\lambda,\nu}-\widetilde{\chi}_{\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U))}\leq\frac{C}{M_{\nu}}

(4.7)

and

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widetilde{T}_{\lambda,\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U))}\leq\frac{C}{M_{\nu}^{2}}\,.

(4.8)

We define then
T_{\lambda,\nu}:=\alpha^{2}\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}^{\ast}\widetilde{T}_{\lambda,\nu}\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U_{\nu})
with
\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}
as in
eq.

A.12
. By unitarity we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert T_{\lambda,\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U_{\nu}))}\leq\frac{\alpha^{2}C}{M_{\nu}^{2}}\lesssim\Lambda^{-2\eta-1}\,.

(4.9)

and

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}^{\ast}\operatorname{Op}\left(\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}\right)\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}T_{\lambda,\nu}-\chi_{\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U_{\nu}))}\leq\frac{C}{M_{\nu}}\lesssim\Lambda^{-2\eta}

(4.10)

but now we invoke
eq.

A.13
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}^{\ast}\operatorname{Op}\left(\widetilde{s_{\lambda}^{\theta}}\right)\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}=\alpha^{2}\operatorname{Op}\left(s_{\lambda}^{\theta}\right)

so that all together we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(h_{\lambda}^{\theta}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)T_{\lambda,\nu}-\chi_{\nu}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(U_{\nu}))}\lesssim\Lambda^{-2\eta}

(4.11)

Appendix A
Review of pseudodifferential operators

Here we recount some facts about pseudodifferential operators. These will mainly be used in order to establish analyticity and bounds of resolvents. In this section,
X
is the position operator on
L^{2}
and
P\equiv-\operatorname{i}\nabla
is the momentum operator on it. With lower case these symbols denote the corresponding real variables: by our convention, under the Fourier transform,
P
becomes a multiplication by the function
p\mapsto p
. Also, for the sake of clarity here the space dimension is
n\in\mathbb{N}
although in the rest of the paper we make use of it with
n=2
.

Let
M\geq 1
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
U\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We study
phase space symbols

a\in C^{\infty}(U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C})
and their regularity classes. To that end, for any multi-indices
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, we define a seminorm

\displaystyle C^{m}_{M,\alpha\beta}(a):=\sup_{x\in U,p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\left|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{p}^{\beta}a(x,p)\right|}{\left(M+\left\lVert p\right\rVert\right)^{m-|\beta|}}

and define the class
S^{m}_{M}
using these seminorms

\displaystyle S^{m}_{M}(U):=\Set{a\in C^{\infty}(U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{p})}{C^{m}_{M,\alpha\beta}(a)<\infty\qquad\forall\alpha,\beta\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}\right)^{n}}\,.

(A.1)

This family of seminorms is separating, so this induces a metric on the space of symbols
S^{m}_{M}(U)
which makes it into a complete locally convex metric vector space. We write
S^{m}_{M}\equiv S^{m}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

To each phase space symbol we associate a
pseudodifferential operator

\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)
, its (Kohn–Nirenberg) quantization. Even if the
x
variable of the symbol
a
is only defined on
U
, we let
\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)
be an operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for convenience. That operator acts on (a dense subspace of) the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
), i.e., we construct a linear map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Op}:S^{m}_{M}(U)\to\mathcal{L}(C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to C^{\infty}(U))

and eventually extend the domain of the operator appropriately. Its action on
u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is prescribed as

\displaystyle\left(\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)u\right)(x):=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\int_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}p\cdot x}a(x,p)\widehat{u}(p)\operatorname{d}{p}

(A.2)

\displaystyle\qquad(x\in U)

where
\widehat{u}
is the Fourier transform of u:

\displaystyle\widehat{u}(p)\equiv\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}p\cdot x}u(x)\operatorname{d}{x}

(A.3)

and formally the integral kernel of
\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)
is given by

\displaystyle U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)(x,y)=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\right)^{n}}\int_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\left(x-y\right)\cdot p}a(x,p)\operatorname{d}{p}\,.

(A.4)

Our main interest in pseudodifferential operators is through the fact that for
a\in S^{2}_{M}(U)
with
\frac{1}{a}\in S^{-2}_{M}(U)
we think of
\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)
as an operator which to some extent behaves very much like the free Laplacian at spectral parameter
M^{2}
, i.e.,

-\Delta+M^{2}\mathds{1}

and, formally restricted to act in
L^{2}(U)
. This is made precise below in
A.5
.

In common abuse of notation, if the risk of confusion is low, when dealing with an explicit formula
f(x,p)
for a symbol
f
, we shall write

\displaystyle\operatorname{Op}\left(f(x,p)\right)

instead of the more cumbersome

\displaystyle\operatorname{Op}\left(\left(x,p\right)\mapsto f(x,p)\right)

and similarly with the seminorms.

A few facts may be readily verified:

Lemma A.1
.

We have

1.

If
b\in C^{\infty}(U)
then
\operatorname{Op}\left(b(x)\right)=b(X)
where
X
is the position operator.

2.

If
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
and
b_{\alpha}:U\to\mathbb{C}
are smooth functions then

\displaystyle\operatorname{Op}\left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}b_{\alpha}(x)p^{\alpha}\right)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}b_{\alpha}(X)P^{\alpha}

where
P_{j}\equiv-\operatorname{i}\partial_{x_{j}}
is the momentum operator in the
j
th direction,
j=1,\dots,n
.

3.

If
b\in\mathbb{C}^{\infty}(U)
then

\displaystyle b(X)\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)=\operatorname{Op}\left(b(x)a(x,p)\right)\,.

(A.5)

4.

If
a\in S^{m}_{M}
and
b\in S^{m^{\prime}}_{M}
then
ba\in S^{m+m^{\prime}}_{M}
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle C^{m+m^{\prime}}_{M,\alpha\beta}(ba)\leq\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha,\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\alpha^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\beta^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}C^{m^{\prime}}_{M,\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}(b)C^{m}_{M,\alpha-\alpha^{\prime},\beta-\beta^{\prime}}(a)\,.

5.

If
a\in S^{m}_{M}
then

\displaystyle P_{j}\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)=\operatorname{Op}\left(p_{j}a(x,p)-\operatorname{i}(\partial_{x_{j}}a)(x,p)\right)

(A.6)

with the first term in
S^{m+1}_{M}
and the second term in
S^{m}_{M}
. We have

\displaystyle C^{m+1}_{M,\alpha\beta}(p_{j}a(x,p))\leq C(m,n)\sup_{\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta}C^{m}_{M,\alpha,\beta-\beta^{\prime}}(a)

and

\displaystyle C^{m}_{M,\alpha\beta}(-\operatorname{i}\partial_{x_{j}}a)=C^{m}_{M,\alpha+e_{j},\beta}(a)\,.

6.

Repeatedly applying the above, if
l
is a symbol of a
differential
operator such that
l\in S^{m^{\prime}}_{M}
and
a\in S^{m}_{M}
then the product of the associated pseudodifferential operators may be decomposed according to the order as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Op}\left(l\right)\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)=\operatorname{Op}\left(la\right)+\operatorname{Op}\left(\varepsilon\right)

(A.7)

where
\varepsilon\in S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}_{M}
. Moreover, the
S^{m+m^{\prime}-1}_{M}
-seminorms of
\varepsilon
are controlled by the
S^{m}_{M}
seminorms of
a
and the
S^{m^{\prime}}_{M}
seminorms of
l
.

7.

If
a\in S^{m}_{M}
depends on analytically on a parameter
\lambda\in\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{C}
for some open
\Omega
, and
\Omega\ni\lambda\mapsto C^{m}_{M,\alpha\beta}(a)
are uniformly bounded, then
\Omega\ni\lambda\mapsto\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)
is an analytic function.

The proof of these facts is standard and is thus omitted.

A standard property of pseudodifferential operators is that

Proposition A.2
.

Let
a\in S^{0}_{M}(U)
. Let
V\subseteq U
be open and relatively compact. Then there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
2N>n
and a constant
C_{n,N}(U,V)<\infty
such that

\left\lVert\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(V))}\;\leq\;C_{n,N}(U,V)\,\Big(\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq 2N}C^{\,0}_{M,\alpha\beta}(a)\Big).

Proof.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\to[0,1])
with
\chi=1
on
V
.

We then apply
[
25
, Theorem 1 on pp. 234]
on the symbol
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni(x,p)\mapsto a(x,p)\chi(x)
.
∎

Corollary A.3
.

Let
a\in S^{-m}_{M}(U)
for
m\geq 0
and
V\subseteq U
be open and relatively compact. Then there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
2N>n
and a constant
C_{n,N}(U,V)
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert P^{\alpha}\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(V))}\leq C_{n,N}(U,V)\sum_{|\tilde{\alpha}|,|\beta|\leq 2N}C_{M,\tilde{\alpha}\beta}^{0}((x,p)\mapsto M^{m-|\alpha|}p^{\alpha}a)M^{|\alpha|-m}

\displaystyle\qquad(\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}:|\alpha|\leq m)\,.

Proof.

Fix
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq m
. By iterating the commutation rule

P_{j}\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)=\operatorname{Op}\left(p_{j}a(x,p)\right)-\operatorname{i}\,\operatorname{Op}\left((\partial_{x_{j}}a)(x,p)\right)

we obtain an expansion

P^{\alpha}\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)\;=\;\operatorname{Op}\left(p^{\alpha}a(x,p)\right)\;+\;\sum_{0\neq\gamma\leq\alpha}c_{\alpha,\gamma}\,\operatorname{Op}\left(p^{\alpha-\gamma}\,\partial_{x}^{\gamma}a(x,p)\right),

(A.9)

for suitable combinatorial constants
c_{\alpha,\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Set

b_{0}(x,p):=p^{\alpha}a(x,p),\qquad b_{\gamma}(x,p):=p^{\alpha-\gamma}\,\partial_{x}^{\gamma}a(x,p)\quad(0\neq\gamma\leq\alpha).

Since
a\in S^{-m}_{M}(U)
, we have

b_{0}\in S^{|\alpha|-m}_{M}(U),\qquad b_{\gamma}\in S^{|\alpha|-|\gamma|-m}_{M}(U)\subset S^{-1}_{M}(U)\quad(0\neq\gamma\leq\alpha),

and because
|\alpha|\leq m
these orders are
\leq 0
. In particular,

b_{0},\;b_{\gamma}\in S^{0}_{M}(U)\qquad(\gamma\leq\alpha).

Define the zero-order symbol

\tilde{a}_{\alpha}(x,p)\;:=\;M^{m-|\alpha|}\,p^{\alpha}a(x,p)\in S^{0}_{M}(U).

Then
b_{0}=M^{|\alpha|-m}\tilde{a}_{\alpha}
, and the symbol estimates in
A.1
(Leibniz rule and the bounds for multiplication by
p
and differentiation in
x
) imply that, for all multi-indices
|\tilde{\alpha}|,|\beta|\leq 2N
,

C^{\,0}_{M,\tilde{\alpha}\beta}(b_{\gamma})\;\leq\;C_{\alpha,m,N}\,M^{|\alpha|-m}\sum_{|\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}|,|\beta^{\prime}|\leq 2N}C^{\,0}_{M,\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}(\tilde{a}_{\alpha}),\qquad 0\leq\gamma\leq\alpha,

for some constant
C_{\alpha,m,N}
depending only on
\alpha,m,N,n
(but not on
a
).

Apply
A.2
to each term in (
A.9
). Using that
V\subseteq U
and the triangle inequality, we get

\displaystyle\left\lVert P^{\alpha}\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(V))}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}|c_{\alpha,\gamma}|\,\left\lVert\operatorname{Op}\left(b_{\gamma}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(V))}

\displaystyle\leq C_{n,N}(U,V)\!\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}|c_{\alpha,\gamma}|\sum_{|\tilde{\alpha}|,|\beta|\leq 2N}C^{\,0}_{M,\tilde{\alpha}\beta}(b_{\gamma})

\displaystyle\leq C_{n,N}(U,V)\,M^{|\alpha|-m}\sum_{|\tilde{\alpha}|,|\beta|\leq 2N}C^{\,0}_{M,\tilde{\alpha}\beta}(\tilde{a}_{\alpha}),

after enlarging
C_{n,N}(U,V)
by a factor depending only on
\alpha,m,N,n
(which we absorb into the notation).

Recalling that
\tilde{a}_{\alpha}(x,p)=M^{m-|\alpha|}p^{\alpha}a(x,p)
gives the stated estimate.
∎

A.1
Elliptic second order operators

Let
M\geq 1
and
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
open be given. We study the symbol

\displaystyle l(x,p)=\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2}+\gamma_{1}(x)\cdot p+\gamma_{0}(x)\qquad(x\in U,p\in\mathbb{R}^{n})

(A.10)

where
\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{1,n},\gamma_{0}
are complex-valued functions on
U
, and
\gamma_{1}(x)\cdot p\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{1,j}(x)p_{j}
. We denote the associated pseudodifferential operator
\mathcal{L}\equiv\operatorname{Op}\left(l\right)
.

Definition A.4
.

We say that
\mathcal{L}
is
elliptic in
S^{2}_{M}(U)
iff

\displaystyle l\in S^{2}_{M}(U)\qquad\land\qquad\frac{1}{l}\in S^{-2}_{M}(U)\,.

The
S^{2}_{M}(U)
seminorms of
l
and the
S^{-2}_{M}(U)
seminorms of
\frac{1}{l}
(there are only finitely many relevant ones) are called the
elliptic
S^{2}_{M}(U)
constants of
\mathcal{L}
.

In the following result, we exhibit a
local
resolvent to
\mathcal{L}
via its elliptic property.

Lemma A.5

(Local elliptic lemma)
.

Let
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two open subset with
V\subseteq U
relatively compact. Let
\mathcal{L}
be elliptic in
S^{2}_{M}(U)
. Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be supported in
V
. Then there exist linear operators
A:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathcal{E}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the following properties:

1.

\mathcal{L}A-\mathcal{E}=\chi
as operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We interpret
A
as a "local" resolvent of
\mathcal{L}
within
V
up to the error
\mathcal{E}
.

2.

A,\mathcal{E}
map
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
into
L^{2}(V)
, i.e.,
\operatorname{supp}Af,\operatorname{supp}\mathcal{E}f\subseteq V
for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

3.

\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha}Af\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq CM^{|\alpha|-2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
for
|\alpha|\leq 2,f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

4.

\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{E}f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq CM^{|\alpha|-1}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
for
|\alpha|\leq 1,f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Here, the constant
C
may be taken to depend only on the elliptic
S^{2}_{M}(U)
constants of
\mathcal{L}
and the
C^{\infty}
seminorms of
\chi
. Moreover, if the coefficients of
\mathcal{L}
depend analytically on a parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, then the operators
A
and
\mathcal{E}
may be taken to depend analytically on
\lambda
.

Proof.

Let
l
be the symbol associated to
\mathcal{L}\equiv\operatorname{Op}\left(l\right)
. Define the symbol

\displaystyle a(x,p):=\frac{\chi(x)}{l(x,p)}\qquad(x\in U,p\in\mathbb{R}^{n})

and the associated operator
A:=\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)
. First, we note
a\in S^{-2}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
thanks to the ellipticity of
\mathcal{L}
. Moreover,
Equation

A.2
implies that for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
Af
will be supported within
V
since
x\mapsto a(x,p)
is supported there, by its construction.

Next, let
\tilde{\chi}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(U)
satisfy
\tilde{\chi}=1
on
V
. Clearly
\tilde{\chi}\in S^{0}_{M}(U)
, and so, thanks to
eq.

A.5
,
\tilde{\chi}l
is in
S^{0}_{M}(U)
. Applying the decomposition
eq.

A.7
on the product of symbols
\tilde{\chi}l
and
a
we find there must exist some symbol
\varepsilon\in S^{-1}_{M}(U)
which obeys

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(X)\mathcal{L}A=\operatorname{Op}\left(\tilde{\chi}la\right)+\operatorname{Op}\left(\varepsilon\right)

with
\varepsilon
the associated symbol, which must be in
S^{-1}_{M}
as the first term is in the top degree. By construction,
\tilde{\chi}la=\tilde{\chi}\chi=\chi
, so we get

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(X)\mathcal{L}A=\chi(X)+\operatorname{Op}\left(\varepsilon\right)\,.

(A.11)

Let
\mathcal{E}:=\operatorname{Op}\left(\varepsilon\right)
.

Since
\mathcal{L}
acts by differentiation and multiplication with smooth coefficient functions, its application does not change the support of a function it acts on. This implies that
\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{L}Af)\subseteq V
for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\tilde{\chi}\mathcal{L}Af=\mathcal{L}Af
. Then
Equation

A.11
implies that
\mathcal{E}f
is also supported within
V
.

Applying
A.3
on
A
and
\mathcal{E}
implies their co-domains are
H^{2}
and
H^{1}
respectively, with the stated estimates.

Next, the definition of
A,\mathcal{E}
via the symbols, implies that if
l
depends analytically on a parameter
\lambda
, then so do the symbols
a,\varepsilon
and hence so do the operators
A,\mathcal{E}
.
∎

A.2
Dilations

For any
\alpha>0
and
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write

\displaystyle\alpha S\equiv\Set{\alpha x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}{x\in S}\,.

We then have a unitary dilation operator
\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
given by

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}\psi\right)(x)\equiv\alpha^{n/2}\psi(\alpha x)\qquad(x\in\mathbb{R}^{n};\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))

(A.12)

and locally
\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}:L^{2}(\alpha S)\to L^{2}(S)
by the same formula. Then,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}^{\ast}X\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha}X\quad{\rm and}\quad\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}^{\ast}P\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}=\alpha P\,.

Further, let
a\in S^{m}_{M}(U)
. Then, with

\displaystyle\alpha U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni(x,p)\mapsto\widetilde{a}_{\alpha}(x,p):=a(x/\alpha,\alpha p)

we have
\widetilde{a}_{\alpha}\in S^{m}_{M}(\alpha U)
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}^{\ast}\operatorname{Op}\left(a\right)\mathfrak{U}_{\alpha}=\operatorname{Op}\left(\widetilde{a}_{\alpha}\right)

(A.13)

as well as

\displaystyle C_{M,\alpha\beta}^{m}\left(\widetilde{a}_{\alpha}\right)\leq\alpha^{|\beta|-|\alpha|}\max\left(\Set{1,\alpha}\right)^{m-|\beta|}C_{M,\alpha\beta}^{m}\left(a\right)\,.

Appendix B
A partition of unity

In this section
\Lambda>0
is a large asymptotic parameter and all other parameters depend on it.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
R>0
be of order
1
and set
N_{\Lambda}:=\lceil\log_{2}(R/\delta)\rceil
,
\delta:=\Lambda^{-1/2+\eta}
for some
\eta>0
small, of order
1
. We also use
\delta_{\nu}:=2^{\nu}\delta
.

There exists a partition of unity, i.e., a sequence
\Set{\chi_{\nu}}_{\nu=0}^{N_{\Lambda}}
where each
\chi_{\nu}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,1]
is smooth and such that

\displaystyle 1=\sum_{\nu=0}^{N_{\Lambda}}\chi_{\nu}\,,

with the following properties:

1.

For
\nu=0
,
\chi_{0}
is equal to
1
on
B_{C\delta}(0)
for some sufficiently large order
1
constant
C
and is supported on
B_{2C\delta}(0)
.

2.

\nu=N_{\Lambda}
:
\chi_{N_{\Lambda}}
is supported in the complement a disc of radius
R
.

3.

\nu=1,\cdots,N_{\Lambda}-1
:
\chi_{\nu}
is supported in an annulus with radii between
2^{\nu-1}\delta
to
2^{\nu+1}\delta
.

4.

For each
\nu
, there exists some
C_{\alpha}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha}\chi_{\nu}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{\alpha}\delta_{\nu}^{-|\alpha|}

where
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\geq 0}
is any multi-index.

5.

There exists another sequence
\Set{\psi_{\nu}}_{\nu}
of smooth functions such that
\psi_{\nu}=1
on
\operatorname{supp}(\chi_{\nu})
, obeying

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial^{\alpha}\psi_{\nu}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{\alpha}\delta_{\nu}^{-|\alpha|}

and such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, no more than, say,
four
values of
\nu
have
x\in\operatorname{supp}(\psi_{\nu})
. We shall also use subsets
U_{\nu}\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
below, each defined so it contains the support of
\psi_{\nu}
. We shall take

\displaystyle U_{\nu}\subseteq\Set{2^{\nu-3}\delta<\left\lVert x\right\rVert<2^{\nu+3}\delta}\qquad(\nu=1,\cdots,N_{\Lambda}-1)

as well as
U_{0}:=B_{2^{3}\delta}(0)
and
U_{N}:=\overline{B_{2^{N-3}\delta}(0)}^{c}
.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Choose a nonincreasing
\vartheta\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty)\to[0,1])
such that

\vartheta(s)=\begin{cases}1,&0\leq s\leq 1,\\
0,&s\geq 2,\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\vartheta\text{ decreases on }(1,2).

We also pick smooth companions
\sigma_{0},\sigma,\sigma_{\infty}\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty)\to[0,1])

with

\sigma_{0}\equiv 1\text{ on }[0,2],\ \ \operatorname{supp}\sigma_{0}\subset[0,3];\qquad\sigma\equiv 1\text{ on }[1/2,2],\ \ \operatorname{supp}\sigma\subset[1/3,3];

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{\infty}\equiv 0\text{ on }[0,1/2],\ \ \sigma_{\infty}\equiv 1\text{ on }[1,\infty).

With
r:=\left\lVert x\right\rVert
, set

\chi_{0}(x):=\vartheta\!\left(\frac{r}{\delta}\right),\qquad\chi_{\nu}(x):=\vartheta\!\left(\frac{r}{2^{\nu}\delta}\right)-\vartheta\!\left(\frac{r}{2^{\nu-1}\delta}\right)\quad(1\leq\nu\leq N-1),

\chi_{N}(x):=1-\vartheta\!\left(\frac{r}{2^{N}\delta}\right).

Then for any
x
(writing
t:=r/\delta
) we have the telescoping identity

\sum_{\nu=0}^{N}\chi_{\nu}=\vartheta(t)+\sum_{\nu=1}^{N-1}\big(\vartheta(2^{-\nu}t)-\vartheta(2^{-(\nu-1)}t)\big)+\big(1-\vartheta(2^{-N}t)\big)=1,

so
\{\chi_{\nu}\}
is a partition of unity with
0\leq\chi_{\nu}\leq 1
.

•

For
\nu=0
,
\chi_{0}\equiv 1
on
B_{\delta}(0)
and
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{0}\subset B_{2\delta}(0)
.
If one wishes the radii to be
C\delta
and
2C\delta
(with
C\gg 1
fixed), simply replace

\chi_{0}(x)
by
\vartheta(r/(C\delta))
.

•

For
1\leq\nu\leq N-1
, since
\chi_{\nu}(x)=\vartheta(s)-\vartheta(2s)
with
s:=r/(2^{\nu}\delta)
,
we have
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\nu}\subset\{\,s\in(1/2,2)\,\}
, i.e.

\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\nu}\subset\big\{\,2^{\nu-1}\delta<\left\lVert x\right\rVert<2^{\nu+1}\delta\,\big\}.

•

For
\nu=N
,
\chi_{N}\equiv 0
on
B_{2^{N}\delta}(0)
and hence

\operatorname{supp}\chi_{N}\subset\{\,\left\lVert x\right\rVert>2^{N}\delta\,\}
. Since
2^{N}\delta\geq R
, this is contained in the
complement of the disc of radius
R
.

By the chain rule and scaling, for any multi-index
\alpha
there exists
C_{\alpha}<\infty
such that

\|\partial^{\alpha}\vartheta(r/a)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C_{\alpha}\,a^{-|\alpha|}.

Therefore, for
1\leq\nu\leq N-1
,

\|\partial^{\alpha}\chi_{\nu}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\partial^{\alpha}\vartheta(r/\delta_{\nu})\|_{\infty}+\|\partial^{\alpha}\vartheta(r/\delta_{\nu-1})\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\alpha}\,\delta_{\nu}^{-|\alpha|},

and the same bound holds for
\nu=0,N
.

Define

\psi_{0}(x):=\sigma_{0}\!\left(\frac{r}{\delta}\right),\qquad\psi_{\nu}(x):=\sigma\!\left(\frac{r}{2^{\nu}\delta}\right)\ \ (1\leq\nu\leq N-1),\qquad\psi_{N}(x):=\sigma_{\infty}\!\left(\frac{r}{2^{N}\delta}\right).

By construction,
\psi_{\nu}\equiv 1
on
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\nu}
for every
\nu
.

To bound the overlap, fix
x
with
t:=r/\delta>0
. For
1\leq\nu\leq N-1
,

x\in\operatorname{supp}\psi_{\nu}\iff 2^{-\nu}t\in[1/3,3]\iff\nu\in\big[\log_{2}t-\log_{2}3,\ \log_{2}t+\log_{2}3\big],

an interval of length
2\log_{2}3<3.2
, hence containing at most
4
integers. Moreover,

\psi_{0}
can only be nonzero when
r\lesssim\delta
and
\psi_{N}
only when
r\gtrsim 2^{N}\delta
; in those regimes the other indices contribute none or at most a couple more, so at any
x
there are no more than four indices
\nu
with
x\in\operatorname{supp}\psi_{\nu}
.

For
1\leq\nu\leq N-1
set

U_{\nu}:=\big\{\,2^{\nu-3}\delta<\left\lVert x\right\rVert<2^{\nu+3}\delta\,\big\},

which are open and satisfy
\operatorname{supp}\psi_{\nu}\subset U_{\nu}
by the choice of supports of
\sigma
.
For the edge indices, take

U_{0}:=\{\,\left\lVert x\right\rVert<2^{3}\delta\,\},\qquad U_{N}:=\{\,\left\lVert x\right\rVert>2^{N-3}\delta\,\}.

These choices match the inclusion required in the statement.
∎

Appendix C
The Landau resolvent for complex magnetic fields

In this section we want to study the resolvent of the Landau Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{B}:=(P-\frac{1}{2}BX^{\perp})^{2}

(C.1)

where
B>0
is the magnetic field strength (eventually we’ll allow
B\in\mathbb{C}
).

Since
H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{B}
is quadratic in both
X
and
P
it may be explicitly diagonalized. An easy route is to write the heat kernel
[
1
]
: For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t>0
,

\exp\left(-tH^{\rm Landau}_{B}\right)(x,y)=\frac{B}{4\pi\sinh\left(Bt\right)}\exp\left(-\frac{B}{4}\coth\left(Bt\right)\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert^{2}-\operatorname{i}\frac{B}{2}x\wedge y\right)\ .

(C.2)

Its Laplace transform yields the following expression for the resolvent kernel at at spectral parameter
z\in\mathbb{C}
, such that
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{z\right\}<B

[
14
, Lemma 5.1]
:

\displaystyle\left(H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{B}-z\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{tz}\exp\left(-tH^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{B}\right)\left(x,y\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{z}{2B}\right)U\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{z}{2B},1,\frac{B}{2}\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert^{2}\right)\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}\frac{B}{2}x\wedge y-\frac{1}{4}B\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert^{2}\right)\,.

(C.3)

Here
U
is Tricomi’s confluent hypergeometric function
[
26
, pp. 129]
:

\displaystyle U(a,1,z):=\frac{1}{\Gamma(a)}\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-tz}t^{a-1}\left(1+t\right)^{-a}\operatorname{d}{t}\qquad(a,z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{a\right\}>0,\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{z\right\}>0)\,.

Since
z\mapsto\Gamma(z)
is meromorphic with simple poles on
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
and
U(a,1,z)
is entire in
a\in\mathbb{C}
and analytic for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]

[
2
]
, we see that
\left(H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{B}-z\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}(x,y)
itself is meromorphic with poles at
z\in B\left(2\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}+1\right)
.

We are interested in setting
B:=\lambda b
where
b>0
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}>0
, and choosing a spectral parameter of the form
z_{\lambda}:=-\lambda^{2}+\mu\lambda
for some
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
. We are seeking an analytic continuation (from
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
to complex values of
\lambda
) of the integral kernel operator

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{\rm diagonal}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\left(H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda b}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}(x,y)

as given explicitly above. The
\Gamma
factor has infinitely many poles at
\lambda\in\mu-b\left(2\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}+1\right)
so that if
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}>\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\mu\right\}-b
we avoid all these poles. The
U
factor is entire in its first argument, and has a branch cut
(-\infty,0]
in its last argument
[
2
]
. Hence for
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}>\max\left(\Set{0,\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\mu\right\}-b}\right)
there are no poles or branch cuts of this integral kernel as a function of
\lambda
. However, it does
not
continue to a bounded linear operator
L^{2}\to L^{2}
; indeed, see
C.2
below.

To remedy this situation, let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact and
Q:=\chi_{K}(X)
. We use the integral kernel above to define an operator which deserves to be denoted by
R^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda b}(z_{\lambda})Q
even for complex
\lambda
since it analytically continues
R^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda b}(z_{\lambda})Q
for real
\lambda
. This operator’s domain, however, is not
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
but rather
QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. For
f\in QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}>\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\mu\right\}-b
, it is given as follows. For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have by
eq.

C.3
:

\displaystyle(R^{\rm Landau}_{B}(z)f)(x):=\int_{y\in K}\operatorname{d}{y}\ K_{B,z}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert)\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\frac{B}{2}x\wedge y}f(y)\,.

(C.4)

with

\displaystyle K_{B,z}(r):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{z}{2B}\right)U\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{z}{2B},1,\frac{B}{2}r^{2}\right)\operatorname{e}^{-\frac{1}{4}Br^{2}}\qquad(r>0)\,.

(C.5)

Proposition C.1
.

Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact and
Q=\chi_{K}(X)
. Let
b,\nu>0
and
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{C}
be such that
\nu\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}>\max\left(\Set{0,\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\mu\right\}-b}\right)
,
B:=\lambda b
,
z_{\lambda}=-\nu\lambda^{2}+\mu\lambda
.

Then, the integral operator
eq.

C.4
is a bounded linear operator
QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
obeying the bound

\displaystyle\left\lVert R^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda b}(z_{\lambda})Q\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\lambda\right|^{\sharp}\exp\left(\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|D\right)^{2}}{b\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}}\right)

(C.6)

where
D:=D_{K}=\sup_{y\in K}\left\lVert y\right\rVert
.

Moreover, if
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}
is sufficiently large, then for all
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have the off-diagonal bound: for any
j=1,2
and
\alpha=0,1
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{S}(X)P_{j}^{\alpha}R^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda b}(z_{\lambda})Q\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\widetilde{C}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\nu}\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}-1\right)-bD\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|\right)\mathrm{dist}(S,K)\right)

(C.7)

where
Q\equiv\chi_{K}(X)
and
\widetilde{C}
is explicit and polynomial in the parameters.

Proof.

Set
A:=1/2-z_{\lambda}/(2\lambda b)\in\mathbb{C}
with
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}>0
and
w:=\frac{1}{2}\lambda b\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert^{2}
with
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{w\right\}>0
(assuming
x\neq y
for the moment).
Then

\displaystyle\left|\Gamma(A)U(A,1,w)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-tw\right)t^{A-1}(1+t)^{-A}\operatorname{d}{t}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{w\right\}\right)t^{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}-1}(1+t)^{-\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}}\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t=0}^{1}t^{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}-1}\operatorname{d}{t}+\int_{t=1}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{w\right\}\right)t^{-1}\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}}+\int_{t=1}^{1/\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{w\right\}}t^{-1}\operatorname{d}{t}+\int_{t=1/\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{w\right\}}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{w\right\}\right)t^{-1}\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}}+\left|\log\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{w\right\}\right)\right|+1

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}}\right)\left(1+\left|\log\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{w\right\}\right)\right|\right)\,.

Moreover, we also have

\displaystyle\left|\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}\frac{\lambda b}{2}x\wedge y\right)\right|=\exp\left(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}b}{2}x\wedge y\right)\,.

Now if
y\in K
then, defining

D:=\ D_{K}\ =\ \sup_{y\in K}\left\lVert y\right\rVert,

and using that

\left|x\wedge y\right|=\left|\left(x-y\right)\wedge y\right|\leq D\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert
we find that the whole kernel is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\left|\left(H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda b}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}(x,y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}}}{4\pi}\left(1+\left|\log\left(\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}b\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert^{2}\right)\right|\right)\times

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}b\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|D\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert\right)\,.

At this point it is convenient to "forget" the fact that
y
only ranges in
K
(as an upper bound) to simplify the invocation of Schur’s test

\displaystyle\left\lVert G\right\rVert^{2}\leq\left(\sup_{x}\int_{y}\left|G(x,y)\right|\operatorname{d}{y}\right)\left(\sup_{y}\int_{x}\left|G(x,y)\right|\operatorname{d}{x}\right)

to

\displaystyle\left\lVert R^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda b}(z_{\lambda})Q\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(QL^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\frac{1}{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}}}{4\pi}\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\operatorname{d}{x}\left(1+\left|\log\left(\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}b\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}\right)\right|\right)\times

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}b\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|D\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right)\,.

Now using

\displaystyle\int_{r=0}^{\infty}r\left(1+\left|\log\left(\alpha r^{2}\right)\right|\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\alpha r^{2}+\beta r\right)\operatorname{d}{r}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{r=0}^{\infty}r\left(1+2\sqrt{\alpha}r+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\alpha}r}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\alpha r^{2}+\beta r\right)\operatorname{d}{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta+1}{\alpha}+\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\left(\alpha+\beta^{2}+\beta+1\right)\operatorname{e}^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2\alpha}}}{\alpha^{3/2}}

we find the estimate
eq.

C.6
.

To get the rate of off-diagonal decay, we return to the integral expression for
\left|\Gamma(A)U(A,1,w)\right|
. Now
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}
is a large parameter:
A=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{-\nu\lambda^{2}+\mu\lambda}{2\lambda b}=\frac{\nu\lambda+b-\mu}{2b}
so
2b\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{A\right\}=\nu\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}+b-\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\mu\right\}
and then, with
\gamma:=\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}
,
r:=\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert
,
w:=\frac{1}{2}\lambda b\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert^{2}
, we get,

\displaystyle\left|\Gamma(A)U(A,1,w)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(1+\frac{1}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\mu\right\}}{2b}}}{t}\exp\left(-\gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}btr^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2b}\log\left(1+\frac{1}{t}\right)\right)\right)\operatorname{d}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(1+\frac{1}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\mu\right\}}{2b}}}{t}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{1}{2}btr^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2b}\log\left(1+\frac{1}{t}\right)\right)\right)\operatorname{d}{t}\times

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\left(\gamma-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{4}r\sqrt{b^{2}r^{2}+4\nu}+\frac{\nu\log\left(\frac{\frac{1}{2}br\left(\sqrt{b^{2}r^{2}+4\nu}+br\right)}{2\nu}+1\right)}{2b}-\frac{br^{2}}{4}\right)\right)\,.

Here we have calculated the minimal value of the function (of
t
) in the exponential and pulled it out of the integral, akin to a Laplace asymptotic calculation. Now since the logarithm term within the exponential is positive and
\sqrt{b^{2}r^{2}+4\nu}\geq 2\sqrt{\nu}
we see that

\displaystyle\left|\left(H^{\mathrm{Landau}}_{\lambda b}-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)^{-1}(x,y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{2b}{\nu+b-\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\mu\right\}}\right)\left(1+\left|\log\left(\frac{1}{2}b\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert^{2}\right)\right|\right)\times

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{\nu}}{2}\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}-1\right)\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\frac{1}{2}b\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|D\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert\right)

but now
\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert\geq\mathrm{dist}(S,K)
so Schur’s test again yields the claimed bound.

For the case
\alpha\neq 0
, we use

\displaystyle\left(P_{j}^{\alpha}R^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda b}(z_{\lambda})\right)(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{i}\partial_{x_{j}}R^{\rm Landau}_{\lambda b}(z_{\lambda})(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{i}\partial_{x_{j}}K_{\lambda b,z_{\lambda}}\left(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert\right)\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}\frac{\lambda b}{2}x\wedge y\right)

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{i}\left(\frac{x_{j}-y_{j}}{\left\lVert x_{j}-y_{j}\right\rVert}K^{\prime}_{\lambda b,z_{\lambda}}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert)-\operatorname{i}\frac{\lambda b}{2}\varepsilon_{ji}y_{j}K_{\lambda b,z_{\lambda}}\left(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert\right)\right)\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}\frac{\lambda b}{2}x\wedge y\right)\,.

The second term is dealt with in the same manner as before, so we only study
K^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle K^{\prime}_{B,z}(r)

\displaystyle=-\frac{Br}{4\pi}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{z}{2B}\right)\operatorname{e}^{-\frac{1}{4}Br^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{z}{2B}\right)U\left(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{z}{2B},2,\frac{1}{2}Br^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}U\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{z}{2B},1,\frac{B}{2}r^{2}\right)\right)\,.

(C.8)

The second term is identical to what was studied above, hence we only need to study the term involving
U\left(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{z}{2B},2,\frac{1}{2}Br^{2}\right)
:

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(A\right)U\left(1+A,2,w\right)=\frac{1}{A}\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-wt}t^{A}\left(1+t\right)^{-A}\operatorname{d}{t}

and we see that this leads to the same asymptotics of the integral as above, for the large parameter
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}
.
∎

To motivate our analysis throughout the paper, we show that we have no hope to continue the Landau resolvent without restricting to a compact set. In fact, we will even allow a relatively compact perturbation of the Landau Hamiltonian.

Proposition C.2
.

Let
v:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
be bounded and of compact support.

Then for
\nu>0,\mu\in\mathbb{C}
fixed,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}>0
, and setting
z_{\lambda}:=-\nu\lambda^{2}+\mu\lambda
,

\displaystyle H^{\rm Landau}_{b\lambda}+\lambda^{2}v(X)-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}

is
not
invertible for all
\lambda
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{R}\mathbbm{e}}\left\{\lambda\right\}
sufficiently large.

Proof.

We shall invoke the Weyl criterion for the spectrum, i.e., we shall show that for any
\delta>0
there exists some
\psi_{\delta}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(H^{\rm Landau}_{b\lambda}+\lambda^{2}v(X)-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\psi_{\delta}\right\rVert\leq\delta\left\lVert\psi_{\delta}\right\rVert\,.

The plan will be to choose define a smooth bump function,
\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}
, supported at a large distance from the support of
v(x)
, in such a way that the magnitude of the phase appearing in the Landau resolvent kernel,

\exp\left(\frac{\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}b}{2}x\wedge y\right)
can be made arbitrarily large
on the support of
\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}
. Then,
\psi_{\delta}:=R_{\lambda b}^{\rm Landau}(z_{\lambda})\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}
, which by
C.1
is well-defined for complex
\lambda
since
\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}
is supported on a compact set, will be large.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\left(H^{\rm Landau}_{b\lambda}+\lambda^{2}v(X)-z_{\lambda}\mathds{1}\right)\psi_{\delta}=\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}+\lambda^{2}v(X)R_{\lambda b}^{\rm Landau}(z_{\lambda})\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}

will be bounded, thanks to the off-diagonal decay bound on
R_{\lambda b}^{\rm Landau}(z_{\lambda})
,
eq.

C.7
.

Let
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be chosen below with
\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert\gg 1
. Let
\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}\geq 0
be a smooth bump function supported within
K:=B_{\beta\left|\lambda\right|^{-1}\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{-1}}(\xi)
for some
\beta>0
and obeying
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}=1
so that
\left\lVert\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}\right\rVert_{2}\lesssim\beta^{-1}\left|\lambda\right|\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert
. We find, by the off-diagonal bound
eq.

C.7
, for sufficiently large
\left|\lambda\right|
. :

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(H^{\rm Landau}_{b\lambda}+\lambda^{2}v(X)-z\mathds{1}\right)\psi_{\delta}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C\beta^{-1}\left|\lambda\right|\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert\ .

To obtain a lower bound on
\left\lVert\psi_{\delta}\right\rVert
, we shall require
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
to be a subset on which the Landau phase factor is large, i.e., for some
c>0

\displaystyle\left|\exp\left(-\operatorname{i}\frac{\lambda b}{2}x\wedge\xi\right)\right|\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}c\left|\operatorname{\mathbb{I}\mathbbm{m}}\left\{\lambda\right\}\right|\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert\right).

(C.9)

Then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\psi_{\delta}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\psi_{\delta}(x)\right|^{2}\operatorname{d}{x}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{x\in U}\left|\left(R_{\lambda b}^{\rm Landau}(z_{\lambda})\widetilde{\psi_{\delta}}\right)(x)\right|^{2}\operatorname{d}{x}
